The pre-rotation key is the identity's recovery path after root compromise and, at the same time, the key whose leak is least recoverable (item 4 below); it is a backstop only while its custody holds. §9.7.4.2 states the events that consume it and the rule that ranks competing chains; §9.12 states the ordered recovery protocol around those events. Its custody MUST be specified with the same rigor as a root member's.

**Custody requirements:**

1. **Generation.** **The criterion: the pre-rotation private key is generated inside the substrate that will hold it, from that substrate's own random source, and no party outside that substrate ever holds it.** That is what the rule requires, and the three readings below are how it applies to the substrates item 4 admits rather than three further rules. **A substrate that generates its own key** — a passkey, a FIDO2 token, an HSM — generates it there, and the SDK supplies no key material and receives none. **A substrate the SDK fills** — the three copyable methods of the headless profile — takes a key the SDK generates on the device from the platform's CSPRNG. **A network HSM under a separate principal is the substrate, not a remote server**, so item 4's HSM row conforms on Desktop and Mobile: what the rule forbids is a party outside the substrate generating the key and sending it, and it does not forbid the substrate sitting across a network the SDK reaches under a grant the operational path cannot assume (item 3a).

2. **Commitment publication.** The pre-rotation commitment (§9.7.4.2 definitions) is a field of the inception event and of every reveal-authorized event; the controller publishes it as part of that event. Only the commitment is published. The public key appears only inside the reveal-authorized event that consumes the commitment.

3. **Storage isolation.** The pre-rotation private key MUST be stored separately from every root member and from the Active Signing Key. It MUST NOT be accessible through the same custody provider or authentication flow used for daily operations. This ensures that compromise of the operational custody path does not compromise the recovery path.

3a. **Recovery-authority residence (normative).** Item 3's storage-isolation requirement constrains *where the recovery authority resides*, not merely which provider object holds the key handle. The **recovery authority** is the minimal secret, key handle, or authorization capability sufficient to recover the pre-rotation private key. It MUST reside in a substrate whose compromise is **independent** of operational-custody compromise: there MUST NOT exist any secret, key handle, or authorization capability reachable **(directly or transitively, following any key-wrapping or key-derivation chain to its root)** from the operational `KeyCustody` provider or the daily-operations authentication flow that — alone or in combination with publicly-stored artifacts (including the encrypted-offline ciphertext of item 4) — suffices to recover the pre-rotation private key. Wrapping the key in an approved cipher (item 4) does NOT by itself satisfy this requirement: the §3 adversary is one who has already compromised operational custody, and if the decryption authority for that cipher is itself reachable from operational custody, the cipher provides zero protection against that adversary. The load-bearing property is **principal-distinctness of the recovery authority**, not the strength of the cipher wrapped around the key.

   **Fail closed — no fallback (normative).** If no substrate satisfying this recovery-authority-residence requirement is available, identity creation MUST fail closed with a typed error. **The same refusal binds at every later reveal**: where no conforming substrate is available for the successor set (item 6), the SDK MUST refuse, with a typed error, to compose a reveal-authorized event at all, and it refuses before it signs — §9.7.4.2 R10 puts the successor-set generation ahead of the persist and the signature, so the refusal lands in the one phase where abort still leaves the standing commitment unconsumed. There is no fallback to co-located operational storage, and no fallback to an in-memory or other dev/test stand-in for the recovery authority. A recovery authority reachable from operational custody is a violation, never a degraded default.

   **A reveal is not a daily operation.** A reveal-authorized event (§9.7.4.2) is a rare, separately-authorized event, not a daily operation. A distinct reveal-time authorization principal — even one that is fully automated (e.g., a separate KMS role assumed only for a rollover or recovery, which the operational path holds no grant to assume) — satisfies this requirement. What does NOT satisfy it is the daily operational signing flow itself being able to reach the pre-rotation key.

   This is an **at-rest / daily-operations** property. It constrains where the recovery authority lives when no reveal-authorized event is in progress. During an authorized rollover or recovery the pre-rotation key signs the event where it resides and is never imported into operational custody; a `RootRecovery` installs a fresh root generated in operational custody, not the revealed key (§9.7.4.2 R3 and R10).

   The fail-closed-no-fallback rule above and the recovery-authority-residence property are canonical and normative here. Items 4 (approved custody methods) and 5 (SDK presentation) below are canonical, and item 4's conformance table states which method conforms on which profile.

4. **Approved custody methods.** Under the key-event log a copy of a pre-rotation key alone does more lasting damage than a copy of the root alone: a root leak is recoverable by a reveal, while a leaked pre-rotation key lets its holder contest the identity at any later date and, held exclusively, take it over and then kill it permanently (the failure-mode table below). The pre-rotation key is therefore the backstop against root compromise and at the same time the key whose leak is least recoverable, and its custody is ranked on two axes with **post-spend destructibility** as the primary one: whether the controller can destroy every copy of a spent key (§9.7.4.2 R5) and, where the substrate supports it, obtain the §9.15 attested destruction; and resistance to theft at rest. Any one method is sufficient **subject to the recovery-authority-residence requirement of item 3a**. Every method holds a P-256 key (§9.5); a method that produces a WebAuthn assertion rather than a raw signature satisfies §9.7.4.2 R3 through the assertion form R3 admits.

   **The custody profile, defined once, with the rule an SDK computes it by.** A profile is a property of the running SDK instance, and the SDK answers two local questions to compute it: does this platform expose a WebAuthn credential API this process may call, and does a human attend this process. **Headless** — no WebAuthn API, whether or not a human attends: a Linux command-line tool, a daemon, an unattended server, and a desktop application on a system whose platform ships no credential provider. **Desktop** — a WebAuthn API through the platform's own credential provider, on a desktop operating system. **Mobile** — the same, on a mobile operating system. **Browser** — a WebAuthn API through a page's `navigator.credentials`. **"Consumer profile" names the union of Desktop, Mobile and Browser**, and it is this section's shorthand rather than a fifth value. The rule turns on the API and not on the human, because item 3a already states that a reveal is a separately authorized event on every profile and the conformance answer turns on whether the profile can hold the substrate and reach it.

   **A profile is a property of a running SDK and never of an identity, and this states what follows.** An identity incepted on a headless host under a copyable method keeps that method's standing pre-rotation key when its `#active` migrates to a phone (`03-identity.md` §3.2.1 case 1), so a consumer-profile device can hold an identity whose recovery authority is copyable and whose residual below is live. **The SDK MUST re-surface the disclosure below whenever it opens an identity whose standing pre-rotation custody type is one of the three copyable values**, on whatever profile it is running, and item 6's next selection is what moves that identity onto a non-copyable method at its next reveal.

   **The consumer profile carries three methods, and every one of them is non-copyable.** A non-copyable substrate is one from which no code path extracts the private key, so the controller never holds a second copy to lose and no party obtains one by copying a file, a share, or a photograph. Alec ruled on 2026-09-10 that self custody — "writing down a key, yes" — is "not a concern", and this table is what that ruling shapes: the copyable methods leave this profile and survive on the headless profile below.

   | Method | Theft resistance | Post-spend destructibility | Description |
   |--------|------------------|----------------------------|-------------|
   | **Passkey (the default)** | High | Destroyable by deleting the credential from the provider; §9.15 software-only confidence | A discoverable WebAuthn credential under the universal relying-party identifier the SDK ships (`ctx.network` by default), held by the platform's passkey provider and synced across that account's devices. The private key is non-exportable, so the controller holds no second copy. The provider's sync fabric may retain an encrypted historical copy the controller cannot verifiably purge; that copy is reachable only by the platform account holder, which makes it a platform-account exposure and never a loose-key exposure. |
   | FIDO2 security token | Highest | Destroyable by on-token credential deletion; attested only where the token attests deletion | A resident WebAuthn credential on a dedicated hardware token (for example a YubiKey 5). The key never leaves the hardware and signing requires physical possession. |
   | HSM-resident key | Highest | Destroyable with §9.15 attested destruction | Key generated in and resident in an HSM under a principal the operational path holds no grant to assume (item 3a). |

   **What the passkey default buys and what it costs.** It buys a substrate the controller cannot copy, which removes the leaked-spent-key failure from this profile by construction rather than by disclosure, and it buys hardware custody on every platform whose enclave speaks P-256 — which is every current one. It costs a dependency on the platform account: losing that account loses the credential, and recovering it is the provider's process rather than SCP's. **The relying-party identifier binds the passkey and never the identity.** A relying party verifies an identifier and a key state and reads no relying-party identifier in either; `SHA-256(rpId)` travels inside the WebAuthn assertion and is public in the log.

   **Headless profile — the copyable methods, and the residual they carry.** A **headless profile** is a deployment that exposes no passkey API and has no attended browser: a Linux command-line tool, a daemon, an unattended server. Those three methods are available there and on no other profile.

   | Method | Theft resistance | Post-spend destructibility | Description |
   |--------|------------------|----------------------------|-------------|
   | Encrypted offline backup | Medium | Destructible only when every physical copy is destroyed | Private key encrypted with AES-256-GCM under a key derived from a user-chosen passphrase via Argon2id (memory: 64 MiB, iterations: 3, parallelism: 4). Stored offline (USB drive, printed QR code, or secure note). The SDK MUST generate the passphrase with at least 128 bits of entropy if auto-generated. |
   | Shamir secret sharing (3-of-5) | Medium | **Not destructible** — shares held by contacts are outside the controller's control | Private key split into 5 shares using Shamir's Secret Sharing (GF(2^8)), any 3 sufficient to reconstruct. Each share is 34 bytes (1 byte index + 33 bytes data). |
   | Paper backup (BIP39 mnemonic) | Lowest acceptable | **Not verifiably destructible** — a copy is one photograph away | Private key encoded as a 24-word BIP39 mnemonic, stored physically. The SDK MUST warn that loss of the paper backup eliminates the recovery path. |

   **The residual these three carry, and this section is its one home.** Each of them yields key bytes, so a copy can outlive the reveal that spent it. Where a copy of a spent pre-rotation key later reaches any party, that party forks below the event that spent the key and reveals the same commitment there. What happens next is the root rule of §9.7.4.2 R6 and nothing else, and it splits on two facts rather than one — whether the controller's own revealing event on that shared prefix carried the standing root's signature, and whether the party holding the copy also holds that same root:

   | The controller's revealing event | The copy holder also holds the shared prefix's root | The copy holder holds no root |
   |---|---|---|
   | carried the standing root's signature (`CommitmentRollover`, or `RootRecovery{CoSigns}`) | both suffixes are rank 1: **the identity is contested, terminal by key material** | controller rank 1, copy holder rank 2: **the controller wins outright** |
   | carried no root signature (`RootRecovery{Lost}`, signed because the root was already gone) | copy holder rank 1, controller rank 2: **the fork wins outright and the identity is lost** | both suffixes are rank 2: **the identity is contested, terminal by key material** |

   **A root signature on the controller's own revealing event never guarantees a win**, and the top-left cell is why: where the leaking party holds the same root the controller co-signed with, that party's own fork carries a signature verifying against the root standing at its predecessor, so both suffixes reach rank 1 and R7 ties them. What `CoSigns` buys against a root-holding leaker is the difference between losing the identity and contesting it. **The SDK MUST state the outcome the row that applies produces, in those terms, at custody selection on this profile**, and §9.7.4.2's fourth worked example traces the bottom-left cell.

   **What the consumer profile's three methods change, stated as the precise claim.** A passkey and a FIDO2 credential are non-exportable and an HSM key is non-extractable, **so the controller never holds a second copy of a spent key and no party obtains one by copying a file, a share, or a photograph** — which is the whole of what non-copyability delivers. It is not a claim that no copy of the key exists anywhere: the passkey row above states that a provider's sync fabric may retain an encrypted historical copy the controller cannot verifiably purge, and that copy is reachable through the platform account and through no other path. **Whoever holds that account holds what the account can assert**, and this section states that reachability as a fact about the substrate; §9.7.4.2 R10's device boundary states what a controller does when a compromise reached a platform account. This section discloses the choice and does not forbid it: a headless controller MAY select a copyable method, having read the disclosure.

   **Method × profile conformance, over every value the custody-type enumeration carries.** A method conforms on a profile when that profile can hold the method's substrate and reach it at reveal time under item 3a. "Conforms" means the SDK MAY offer the method on that profile; "does not conform" means the SDK MUST NOT offer it there. **The rows below cover all nine values §9.7.4.2's definitions fix**, so an SDK gets an answer for every value rather than for the six this table once carried.

   | Custody type | Conforms as a pre-rotation substrate on |
   |---|---|
   | `Passkey` (the default) | Desktop and Mobile, through the platform's credential provider. Browser, from a page whose origin sits under the relying-party identifier the credential was registered against and from no other origin, because WebAuthn admits an assertion only where the calling origin's effective domain is that relying-party identifier or a registrable-domain suffix of it. Not Headless, which is the profile defined by the absence of the API |
   | `Fido2Token` | All four. A USB or smartcard reader on a headless host, USB or NFC on a desktop, NFC or USB-C on mobile, and an external authenticator through a browser's WebAuthn API |
   | `Hsm` | Headless, Desktop and Mobile — the headless profile's primary method, and a network HSM under a separate principal on the other two. Not Browser: a page holds no credential for a principal its own origin cannot reach |
   | `EncryptedOfflineBackup`, `ShamirShares`, `PaperBackup` | Headless only. Alec ruled on 2026-09-10 that self custody is "not a concern", and these three leave the consumer profile under that ruling and survive here with the residual above disclosed |
   | `SecureEnclave`, `AndroidKeystore` | No profile. The operational key `#active` sits in that same platform keystore under that same application principal, so item 3a's principal-distinctness test fails by construction; a deployment that can establish a separate principal in hardware uses the `Hsm` row |
   | `Software` | No profile. It is the operational path's own substrate and it is copyable, so item 3a rejects it as a recovery authority on every profile |

   **A reveal may be human-attended on every profile, the headless one included**, so no row turns on whether a human is present when the reveal runs: item 3a already states that a reveal-authorized event is a rare, separately authorized event and not a daily operation. What a row turns on is whether the profile can hold the substrate and reach it under item 3a.

   Every conforming answer is still subject to item 3a: the SDK MUST reject a selection whose recovery authority is reachable from the profile's operational custody, whatever this table says. That test, not the table, is what rejects a mobile HSM arrangement whose KMS grant the mobile application's operational signing flow can assume. **It also decides the passkey rows:** a passkey the daily operational signing flow can itself assert against is co-located custody, so the SDK MUST hold the pre-rotation passkey as a credential that flow holds no path to.

5. **SDK presentation.** At identity creation, and again for the successor key before every reveal-authorized event (§9.7.4.2 R10), the SDK MUST:
   a. Generate the pre-rotation keypair (item 1).
   b. Present the user with custody options (item 4, both axes shown).
   c. Guide the user through the selected custody method.
   d. **Confirm the key is reachable, and the confirmation differs by substrate rather than the step differing.** For an offline method, require the user to re-enter or re-scan the backup. **For a non-copyable substrate — a passkey, a FIDO2 token, an HSM — take one test signature from the credential over a value the SDK chose and verify it against the public key the commitment covers**, which confirms that the credential exists, that this SDK can reach it, and that it signs under the key the commitment names.
   e. Sign and publish the event that carries the commitment — the inception event at creation (§9.7.4.2 R2), or the reveal-authorized event that fixes the successor commitment (§9.7.4.2 R4) — only after step d's confirmation succeeds. **The gate is step d's confirmation on every substrate**, so it has a defined precondition for a passkey exactly as it has one for a mnemonic.
   f. Destroy the pre-rotation private key from the creating device's memory. **On a non-copyable substrate no private key ever entered that memory, so this step has nothing to destroy and the SDK discharges it by holding no key material to destroy** — stated so a reader does not take an unconditional destroy step as evidence that the key passed through the SDK.

6. **The successor key at every reveal.** Every reveal-authorized event fixes the next pre-rotation commitment inside the same event (§9.7.4.2 R4). The successor key follows items 1, 3a, 4, and 5b–5d before the event is signed; §9.7.4.2 R10 states when in the ceremony, and §9.7.4.2 R5 states the destruction of the spent key.

7. **Custody status tracking.** The SDK SHOULD periodically prompt the user to verify their pre-rotation key backup is still accessible (e.g., every 6 months). This is a client-level reminder, not a protocol-level enforcement — the protocol cannot verify that an offline backup still exists.

8. **Standing-key retention.** The controller MUST retain every standing pre-rotation private key — each key's backup under item 4 — from the moment the commitment list is fixed until a reveal-authorized event supersedes that list, on every code path. The obligation covers every member of the set and ends for the whole set at that event, whether the reveal named the member or not; §9.7.4.2 R5 then destroys the whole set. Loss of the standing key forecloses both reveal-authorized event kinds, because the standing root alone cannot fix a new commitment (§9.7.4.2 R1); the failure-mode table below states the outcome. Item 7's periodic verification is the client-level check on this obligation.

**Failure modes.** Each row names the keys the attacker holds and the outcome the rules of §9.7.4.2 produce. **Every outcome below follows from the root rule of R6 and from nothing else**: a suffix whose revealing event carries the signature of the root standing at that event's predecessor is rank 1, a suffix whose revealing event carries no such signature is rank 2, a suffix that reveals nothing is rank 3, and a lower rank number wins. No cosignature enters any row: a witness watches and reports, and §9.7.4.3 states what a relying party may read a cosignature for. Where a row says the root cannot be recovered by key material, the person establishes a new identity and each context's admins remove the old identity and admit the new one — after confirming the head from a relay in the fallback set (§9.7.4.2 definitions), which the hostile identity's service record does not name (§9.7.4.2 R4). The social recovery of `03-identity.md` §3.3 does not apply to those rows, because §3.3 re-establishes custody of the same identity. "P" below means enough of the next set to reveal. **A 1-of-1 next set is the single-custody-loss configuration:** for a personal identity P is one key, so one loss of one key hands a party everything every P row below needs. A next threshold of two or more, with the members in independent custody, raises the precondition of every one of those rows from one loss to that many, and §9.7.4.2 R3 already permits it — `MAX_NEXT_SET_SIZE` is 16 and the only bound on the threshold is that it be at least the standing root's threshold. This sentence states what an existing mechanism costs and restricts nothing.

- **Pre-rotation backup lost, root intact.** The identity keeps operating. The controller can sign no `CommitmentRollover` and no `RootRecovery`, because both require a reveal, and the root alone cannot fix a new commitment (§9.7.4.2 R1); it also cannot abandon, because abandonment is a reveal-authorized event. The SDK MUST warn that root recovery and retirement are no longer possible. Item 8 is the obligation whose breach produces this state.
- **Pre-rotation backup lost, root compromised.** Neither party can reveal, so both suffixes are rank 3 and the tie is pending (§9.7.4.2 R7). No later event ends it, because ending it takes a reveal and no party holds one. The root cannot be recovered by key material.
- **P copied, root intact and trusted.** The controller wins outright and the identity is not contested. §9.7.4.2's second worked example traces it event by event, and it is the case Alec's root-wins ruling of 2026-09-07 decided.
- **P copied, root lost.** Both parties sign a `RootRecovery{Lost}` and neither carries a standing-root signature, so both suffixes are rank 2, the identity is contested, and the tie is terminal by key material: each side's reveal consumed the commitment the comparison read.
- **P exclusively the attacker's, whatever the state of the root.** The attacker reveals and the controller cannot: the attacker's suffix is rank 2 at worst against the controller's rank 3, and the attacker's `RootRecovery{Lost}` is adopted uncontested. From that point the attacker is the standing root and holds every power of the controller — including abandonment under the commitment its own recovery fixed — so **the attacker kills the identity permanently**, and the identity is dead. Item 3a exists to keep this case from arising through operational-custody compromise; no key-material mitigation exists once the key is exclusively the attacker's, and this row is why the custody table ranks destructibility first.
- **A copy of P forks before the controller's abandoning rollover.** The controller holds its root and publishes an abandoning `CommitmentRollover`, which R4 requires the standing root to co-sign; a holder of a copy of P forks before it with a `RootRecovery{Lost}` revealing the same commitment. The controller's suffix is rank 1 and the copy holder's is rank 2, so **the abandonment is adopted and takes effect**. The copy holder's fork loses at every relying party, and no ordering of events on the controller's side is needed to reach that outcome.
- **Root compromised, P held only by the controller.** The controller wins wherever the attacker forked, and the recovery's snapshot replaces every install the attacker made. §9.7.4.2's first worked example traces it.
- **Root compromised and P copied.** The attacker holds a threshold of the root and enough of the next set, so it produces a `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` at rank 1; the controller, still holding its own root, produces one too. Both suffixes are rank 1, the identity is contested, and the tie is terminal by key material. This is the unrecoverable case the signing-authority matrix of ADR-063 names, and Alec's reason for accepting it, verbatim: "If an attacker has the root, it's GG."
- **Root compromised, owner recovered, and a copy of a spent pre-rotation key later leaks — the residual this design carries.** A party that obtains that copy forks below the event that spent the key and reveals the same commitment there. **Item 4's four-cell table above is the one home of the outcomes**, and this row cites it rather than restating it: the split runs on whether the owner's own revealing event carried the standing root's signature and on whether the leaking party also holds that same root, and a `CoSigns` recovery against a root-holding leaker ends in a terminal contest rather than in a win. §9.7.4.2's fourth worked example traces the cell in which the fork wins outright. **On the consumer profile the controller holds no copy of a spent key to lose**, because item 4's three methods are non-copyable; item 4 states what that claim covers and what it does not, and it names the platform account as the path by which a passkey provider's retained encrypted copy stays reachable. Destruction that reaches every backup medium (item 4's destructibility axis, §9.7.4.2 R5) is what prevents the leak on the headless profile, and R5's destruction, item 5f, and item 5d each reach only the controller's own copies.
- **Two devices both holding the root, no attacker.** Both suffixes are rank 3 and the tie is pending; the controller's exit spends a commitment, and R10's avoidance is cheaper. §9.7.4.2's third worked example traces both.

**Partial-publish recovery.** A reveal-authorized event has one irreversible step, the reveal; §9.7.4.2 R10 states the ceremony order, the retention invariant that makes destruction safe, the definition of confirmed publication, and the typed recovery handle that is persisted before publication.

### 9.7.4.2 Root-Authority Recovery and Fork Precedence

This section states how an identity's root recovers from compromise and how a verifier decides between two key-event chains that diverge from a shared prefix. §9.7.4.1 supplies the recovery key's custody rules; this section states the events that consume that key and the rule that ranks competing chains. Every statement below is a verifier obligation or a controller obligation. The identity spec and the identity ADRs cite this section and do not restate its rules. **Every rule of this section decides an event's acceptability from signed bytes alone.** Validity under R1 through R4 and rank under R6 are functions of the chain's own bytes, so a verifier reaches every verdict below with no witness, no clock, and no network beyond fetching the chain. **A witness cosignature enters no rule of this section** (§9.7.4.3 states what a relying party may read one for). **No rule of this section reads a clock, and no rule outside it reads one about this section's objects.** The wall clock enters the freshness test the definitions state over corroboration, and corroboration is a fact the SDK records about how it read a head and surfaces to the human; it withholds no act. §9.14 governs clock skew for the rest of the protocol.

This section governs a **non-delegated** identifier — one whose key state carries the all-zero `delegator` placeholder, which is the only field that expresses the claim (definitions). For a delegated identifier the delegator's chain anchors the delegate's establishment events by a key-event seal, so precedence reads the delegator's chain, and ADR-064, the forthcoming specification of the cooperative-delegation events, states that model. Until it lands, R3's bullet on a nonzero `delegator` rejects every chain that claims delegation.

**Definitions.**

- The **root** of an identity is a **root set** — an ordered list of at most `MAX_ROOT_SET_SIZE` public keys (§9.18.17) — with a **signing threshold** t. A **root signature** is an **indexed signature group**: the signed preimage names the list of root-set indices that sign and the signature form of each named index's slot (below), the signature field carries exactly one slot per named index in index order laid out by that index's form, every indexed signature MUST verify against the member at its index, and the named indices MUST number at least t and MUST be distinct. Every statement below that says a root "signs" means a root signature. A personal identity is the 1-of-1 case: one key, threshold one, one index, one slot. An organization sets a list and a threshold so that no one officer acts alone. **Any slot of any indexed signature group MAY carry a WebAuthn assertion** in place of a raw signature, and the signature-form bullet below states both layouts and the checks each one takes. The assertion form exists because the SDK's default root custody is a passkey (§9.7.4.1 item 4), and a WebAuthn authenticator signs its own envelope rather than a preimage handed to it. **It is admitted at every group and not at the root group alone** — the reveal group below, the installed-set K′ group of R3, and the standing-root group — because §9.7.4.1 item 4 names a passkey as the default substrate of a pre-rotation key as well as of a root member, and K′ is generated in operational custody that may also be a passkey. The **standing root** at a position in a chain is the root set and threshold the latest root-installing event at or before that position installed: the inception event installs the first, and a `RootRecovery` installs each later one.
- The **signature form** of a slot is a one-byte discriminator, one per slot, carried in a **signature-form list** that the event's preimage binds beside the signer index list of the group that slot belongs to. **Two values are defined, and a verifier MUST reject a chain carrying any other** (`Invalid{at_event}`, R3):
  - **`0x01`, the raw form.** The slot is the 64-byte raw ECDSA signature §9.5 fixes — `r` then `s`, each a 32-byte big-endian integer, low-`s` normalized by the signer (§9.5) — verified against the key at the slot's index over the event's §9.5.1 preimage digest.
  - **`0x02`, the WebAuthn assertion form.** The slot is `BE32(len(authenticatorData)) || authenticatorData || BE32(len(clientDataJSON)) || clientDataJSON || signature`, where each `BE32` is the 4-byte big-endian length prefix §9.5.1 fixes for a variable-length field, `len(authenticatorData)` is at most `MAX_AUTHENTICATOR_DATA_BYTES` and `len(clientDataJSON)` at most `MAX_CLIENT_DATA_JSON_BYTES` (§9.18.17), and `signature` is 64 raw bytes. **A WebAuthn authenticator emits that signature as a DER-encoded `ECDSA-Sig-Value`** (Web Authentication Level 2, section 6.5.6, COSE algorithm −7), so the **signer** converts it to `r || s` and low-`s` normalizes it (§9.5) before it places the slot. A verifier reads 64 raw bytes at that position and never DER.
- **What a verifier does with an assertion slot, stated as five obligations and one prohibition.** It parses `clientDataJSON` as RFC 8259 JSON and **MUST reject a document carrying a duplicate member name at any nesting level**, because JSON parsers differ over which duplicate wins and two honest verifiers holding one chain would otherwise disagree about one event's validity. It **MUST reject** the slot unless the `type` member equals `"webauthn.get"`. It **MUST reject** the slot unless the `challenge` member equals the unpadded base64url encoding of `"SCP-KEY-EVENT-V1:" || D`, where `D` is that event's own §9.5.1 preimage digest and the prefix is the WebAuthn-challenge domain §9.18.2 registers; **that prefix is what makes a sign-in assertion unusable as a key-event signature**, because a sign-in ceremony's challenge is a value its relying party chose and carries no such prefix. It **MUST reject** the slot unless the user-presence flag is set in `authenticatorData`. It then verifies the 64-byte signature against the key at the slot's index, as an ECDSA-with-SHA-256 signature over the message `authenticatorData || SHA-256(clientDataJSON)`, which is the message WebAuthn defines and is not `D`. **It records the `rpIdHash` that `authenticatorData` carries and the `origin` that `clientDataJSON` carries, and it draws no claim about the identity from either**: both say which credential signed and where it was asked, and what a relying party verifies is the identifier and the key state. The clause this replaces told a verifier to read no other field of `clientDataJSON`, which forbade the `type` check section 7.2 of the Web Authentication specification requires; the prohibition is on drawing an identity claim from a field, never on parsing one.
- An **operational key** is a signing key other than a root member — currently the Active Signing Key `#active`, which is the identity's one operational role.
- The **key algorithm** of a key is a one-byte discriminator that every state-carrying event carries beside that key, and that the inception event carries for every key it installs and for every commitment it fixes. **Its name is `KeyAlgorithm` and every SDK binding carries that name and its variant names unchanged**, exactly as R3's four kind names and R14's six verdict names bind: the field exists so that a later revision registers a second variant, and four bindings inventing four names for it would each register that variant under a different name. **Exactly one variant is registered for v1**, `EcdsaP256Sha256` = `0x01` (`KEY_ALGORITHM_ECDSA_P256_SHA256` in §9.18.17): ECDSA on P-256 with SHA-256, whose verification key is the 33-byte SEC1 compressed point and whose signature is the 64-byte raw `r || s` form (§9.5). A verifier MUST reject a chain carrying an algorithm value it does not recognize (`Invalid{at_event}`). **The field exists from the first version of the schema and carries one value, for a reason the identifier construction forces:** the inception event's preimage is frozen because its digest is the identifier, so an algorithm discriminator absent from the first schema could never appear in an existing identity's initial record, and adding a second algorithm later would then rename every identity that wanted it. The field is therefore what lets a later revision register a second algorithm while every existing identifier stands unchanged.
- Every key event is an **establishment event**: each of the four kinds (R3) installs, restates, or commits the identity's key material. The root signs establishment events and nothing else; operational keys sign content and attestations and never sign an establishment event. That separation is what lets a compromised operational key be retired without the root's cooperation being in question, and what keeps the root cold.
- An event's **sequence** is its predecessor's sequence plus one, and the inception event's sequence is zero; a verifier rejects an event whose sequence is anything else (R3). A **position** in a chain is that sequence, so chain order and position order are one order, and every rule below that says "position" means the sequence.
- The **community relay list** is a single artifact the SDK ships: a fixed list of relays, each entry declaring the **operator identity** that runs it, published with the SDK and identical in every binding of one release. `18-addressability-and-deployment.md` §18.5.1 defines the artifact and its entry shape; this definition and that section are the only two homes, and no rule draws the list from a bootstrap priority order. **The artifact carries two roles with two owners.** It is the source of the fallback set below, which is a reachability property, and it is the SDK's default **recognized set** below, which decides whose cosigned heads a party reads as evidence. Neither role decides whether an event is valid or how a fork ranks: R6 reads the chain's own bytes and reads no list. The **fallback set** for an identity is the set of entries of the community relay list the identity's own service record (`03-identity.md` §3.10.13) does not name, **and where that set is empty it is the community relay list itself**; this definition is the one home of both halves. The exclusion exists because the holder of the designated key chose the relays that record names, so a source the record names is a source that key's holder chose. **The non-empty rule closes the exclusion's own failure:** a party holding the designated key names every entry of the community relay list in one service record at no cost, §3.10.13 bounds no entry count, and the subtraction would then leave nothing — so R4's confirmation before social action could never be discharged, and the identity's own holder would hold a permanent veto over its removal from every context and over any relying party ever treating its tokens as revoked. With the fallback set falling back to the whole list, the confirmation is discharged against a source the record does name, which is weaker evidence than the exclusion asks for and is evidence a relying party can obtain; the exclusion delivers what it promises in every case where a source outside the record exists, and delivers a reachable source in the case where none does. **Two readers exclude nothing.** A reader holding no accepted service record for the identity takes every entry of the community relay list as that identity's fallback set, and re-evaluates the definition only once it accepts a service record; R11's first-contact floor is the floor that reader applies, so both of R11's sources are fallback-set entries and a list carrying fewer than two entries under distinct declared operators fails that floor closed. R10's ceremony floor likewise excludes nothing: a service record that names a community-list entry leaves that entry in the ceremony's fallback set, because the identity pointed at an operator it did not choose. **The set is not a priority order and no rule reads one:** a relay a deployer configured, a relay a peer advertised, and a relay the identity's service record names are all outside the fallback set unless the community relay list carries them.
- The **next set** is the list of at most `MAX_NEXT_SET_SIZE` pre-rotation public keys (§9.18.17) whose commitments the chain fixes (below), with a **next threshold** n. A **reveal** is an indexed signature group over the next set: the preimage names at least n distinct next-set indices and the signature form of each named index's slot (above), the revealing event carries the public key for each named index, and the signature field carries one slot per named index in index order laid out by that index's form, each verifying against the revealed key at that index. The **pre-rotation key** P names one member of that set; each is a one-shot authorizer that signs exactly one reveal-authorized event and is then spent and destroyed (R5), and never becomes a root member or any other key. The name is retained; this definition, not the name, governs.
- The **custody type** of a key names the substrate that holds its private half. The enumeration is fixed here, and every other section — the identity spec's custody-migration protocol (`03-identity.md` §3.2.1) included — references it rather than restating it. Its values are `Passkey`, `Fido2Token`, `Hsm`, `SecureEnclave`, `AndroidKeystore`, `EncryptedOfflineBackup`, `ShamirShares`, `PaperBackup`, and `Software`. §9.7.4.1 item 4 states which of them the SDK may offer for a pre-rotation key on which profile, and names `Passkey` as the default for a root member and for a pre-rotation key alike. **Every value names a substrate that holds a P-256 key**, so no custody type is barred at a root member and a verifier rejects none on that ground; what constrains a selection is §9.7.4.1 item 3a's principal-distinctness test, which no verifier can check from signed bytes and which the SDK enforces at selection.

  **The custody type is a declaration its author chose, and every consumer reads it as `Software` unless a platform proof verifies.** Alec ruled that on 2026-08-25, verbatim: "either the platform proof is attached and verified, or the custody model reads as software no matter what string was written"; §27.4.4 states the four clauses of that ruling and this definition adds nothing to them. **The key state carries no platform proof**, and no field of it does, so today every consumer of a key state's custody type reads `Software` for every key — at a trust evaluation, at an admission gate, in a diagnostic report, and in a value shown to a human, which §27.4.4 clause 4 names as four instances of a consumer. A verifier still rejects no chain on the value, because R3 admits every one of the nine. **A key state whose declared value another party read as a hardware rating would give the strongest rating to a claim nothing checks**, which is the outcome the ruling exists to foreclose.

  **Every key the key state names carries a custody type, and that includes the next set.** A root member, each operational key, and each member of the next set whose commitment the chain fixes carry one value each. The next set's members carried a key algorithm and no custody type in a prior revision, which left the substrate holding the identity's recovery authority — the key §9.7.4.1 item 4 calls the one whose leak is least recoverable — the one substrate no other party could read anything about. What that value is worth is what the paragraph above states: it reads as `Software` until a platform proof verifies.
- The **key state** of an identity is: the standing root, each of its members carrying a **custody type** (above), one value per member; every operational key by role (`#active`) and by custody type, one value per key; **every key that has ever appeared on the chain**, each carrying both the role it held — a root-set membership or a named operational role — and one of the four conditions of §9.7.1 (`current`, `Superseded`, `Retired`, or `Compromised{from: N}`), so that the set of keys that ever held a named role is computable from the snapshot alone and no former root member is admissible in an operational role; **each member of the next set**, carrying a key algorithm and a **custody type** exactly as an installed key does (above); the **witness set** — at most `MAX_WITNESS_SET_SIZE` relays (§9.18.17) the controller designates to cosign the identity's log head — with the **witnessing interval** (`u32` seconds, bounded below by `MIN_WITNESSING_INTERVAL` and above by `MAX_WITNESSING_INTERVAL` (§9.18.17); the interval at which each designated witness re-cosigns the head, the input to the freshness test below, and the floor on the controller's self-observation cadence under R10); the **service-key designation** — which operational key signs the identity's service record (`03-identity.md` §3.10.13), named by role and covered by the root signature that covers every other field here; and, for a delegated identifier, the **delegator** (a 32-byte identifier, or the all-zero placeholder for a non-delegated identity). **The key state carries no relay endpoint, no private-state location, and no capability URI.** Every transport and service field lives in the service record instead, which the designated key signs and which ADR-063, the inception-derived key-event-log identity substrate, splits off the identifier record for this reason: a controller changes a relay endpoint by writing a service record under an operational key, appending no key event and warming no root. Each of the fields above is in the inception event's preimage because the identifier freezes that preimage: the initial witness set is what a first-contact resolver reads to learn where cosigned heads for this identity live, custody type is what a consumer reads under §27.4.4's clauses and reads as `Software` until a platform proof verifies (above), the key algorithm is what a verifier reads before it parses a key, and delegation must be declared from the first event because a delegated chain's precedence reads the delegator's chain. **The inception fixes the initial value of each field; it does not freeze the field.** Every state-carrying event carries the whole key state, so a later `KeyState` changes the witness set, the witnessing interval, the service-key designation, the operational roles, and each key's condition, exactly as R8 describes. The one field the inception freezes for the life of the identity is the delegator, because a delegated chain's precedence reads a different chain and a verifier must know from the first event which chain that is.
- A **state-carrying event** carries the complete key state. Three kinds carry state: the inception event, a `KeyState`, and a `RootRecovery`. **The derived key state at any position is the key state the latest state-carrying event at or before that position carries** (R8); no earlier event contributes.
- A **key-event seal** is a digest, carried by a `KeyState` event, of another identity's key event — the construction by which a delegator anchors a delegate's establishment event. Its preimage is `SHA-256("SCP-KEL-SEAL-V1:" || anchored_event_preimage_digest)` (§9.18.2); one seal anchors one event. A seal anchors key events only; it plays no part in content verification. **R3 rejects every chain whose `delegator` is nonzero until ADR-064, the cooperative-delegation model, lands, so the seal has no live reader today, and the reason it ships now is the one §9.5.1 states about every added field:** adding a field to a preimage requires incrementing that preimage's separator version, which invalidates every signature made under the old one. A seal added after ADR-064 would therefore be a flag day for every chain in existence, and shipping the field now costs one unread digest per `KeyState` instead.
- A **pre-rotation commitment** is `SHA-256("SCP-PREROTATION-COMMITMENT-V1:" || pre_rotation_public_key)` over one member of the next set; the chain fixes the list of commitments and the next threshold together. The public key is a fixed-length 33-byte SEC1 compressed P-256 point (§9.5), so under §9.5.1 it carries no length prefix.
- The **standing commitment** at a position in a chain is the commitment list and next threshold fixed by the latest commitment-fixing event at or before that position — the inception event (R2) or a reveal-authorized event (R4).
- A **reveal-authorized event** carries a reveal against the standing commitment at the event's predecessor: each revealed key's commitment MUST be the member of the standing list at the index the preimage names. **The threshold that reveal is checked against is the next threshold of the standing commitment** — the one fixed alongside the list the reveal consumes — and never the threshold the event itself fixes. The threshold an event fixes governs only the next reveal, against the list that event fixes. Rationale: a reveal checked against its own event's threshold would let a holder of one member of a 2-of-3 standing set name one index, declare its own successor threshold 1, and take the identity over on a threshold the previous controller never set.
- A **witness** is a relay an identity's key state designates to cosign that identity's log head. A **cosigned head** is one witness's signed statement that it held this identity's chain at one head at one moment; a **conflict statement** is one witness's signed statement that two chains for one subject were offered to it. §9.7.4.3 states both objects' fields, their signature preimages, their addresses, and the one check a witness applies before it cosigns. **Neither object enters any rule of this section.** A relying party reads a cosigned head as evidence of three things and no fourth: how stale the head it holds may be, that recognized operators were serving this chain at a stated time when it first contacted the identity, and — together with a second cosigned head or a conflict statement — that a party offered two divergent chains for one subject.
- **An operator's own chain is resolved by a non-recursive floor, and this bullet is the one home of that rule.** A party that must resolve a community-relay-list operator's chain — to verify that operator's cosigned head, or to verify its relay proof of control (§9.7.4.3) — resolves it under R1 through R4, and under R6 and R7 where it holds two divergent chains for that operator; the trust anchor is the identifier the operator's own community-relay-list entry declares. **The floor is that entry plus one other entry of the list under a different declared operator**, counted by list membership and reachability, and a party reaching only one of the two resolves no operator chain and reads neither object. **That floor carries no proof-of-control term, and the omission is the point:** applying R11's floor unchanged here would make counting the first source require resolving an operator's log, which would require two proven sources, each requiring another log, and the recursion would not terminate. R11, §9.7.4.3, and `18-addressability-and-deployment.md` §18.5.1 cite this bullet and restate it nowhere.
- A relying party's **recognized set** is the list of operator identities whose cosigned heads that party reads as evidence about the identities it resolves. The set is the party's own policy, held locally, and it defaults to the community relay list above. It is never derived from any suffix's own key state, because that suffix's author chose what its key state names. **A cosignature from an operator outside the set counts toward nothing and is not a defect**: it is a valid signature by a party this relying party did not choose to read, and it attributes nothing to the subject.
- The **staleness window** at a relying party is `WITNESS_STALENESS_INTERVALS × witnessing_interval` (§9.18.17), where `witnessing_interval` is the value the derived key state carries. Every key state carries that field whether or not the identity designates any witness, and R3 bounds it, so the window is defined for every identity. **A relying party MAY apply a shorter window of its own**, because the subject writes `witnessing_interval` into its own chain: the value the key state carries is a floor on that subject's own cosigning cadence and never a bound on the reading party's judgement. Which window a party applies changes nothing any rule reads, because the paragraph below states that no rule reads corroboration at all.
- **A relying party's adopted head is corroborated when either of two independent sources says so, and this definition is the one home of both.** **Source one, a witness read:** the party holds a cosigned head over this chain at a sequence at or above its adopted head, signed by an operator its recognized set carries, whose `observed_at` sits inside the staleness window and no further in the future than §9.14's clock-skew tolerance. A cosigned head meeting those conditions is **fresh**. **Source two, a two-relay read:** within the staleness window, the party itself obtained a head of this chain at a sequence at or above its adopted head from at least two entries of the community relay list under distinct declared operators, each of which discharged the proof-of-control obligation its entry carries by serving a **relay proof of control** the party verified (§9.7.4.3 states that object's fields, its preimage, and its freshness bound; R11 states the floor it feeds; `18-addressability-and-deployment.md` §18.5.1 states the obligation an entry carries). **The two sources are not equivalent and this states the difference rather than implying it.** A witness read is stamped by the witness's own clock and carries an accountable signature, so it survives being relayed to a third party and it names an operator if it lies. A two-relay read is stamped by the asking party's own clock, so it needs no other party's clock to be right, and it is not portable: it is evidence to the party that performed it and to nobody else. **Either one alone corroborates.** A party that holds neither records that it holds neither and surfaces it to the human, and it withholds nothing on that record: corroboration says how this party came to hold the head it holds, and it judges neither the subject nor the head.
- **No rule reads corroboration or freshness — no rule of this section, and no rule of any other.** A party derives the same key state, reaches the same rank, and returns the same verdict whatever the age of the evidence it holds, and a party holding none reaches all three too. **Corroboration is information the SDK records and surfaces, and it withholds no act and closes no gate.** A design in which the absence of corroboration withheld an act would withhold that act on a property of the reading party's own network, and it would restore the missing-cosignature gate the 2026-09-10 watch-and-report ruling removed, wearing the name of a second source. **An identity that designates no witnesses produces no cosigned head and is corroborated by the two-relay read alone.** A party that reaches fewer than two community-relay-list entries under distinct declared operators corroborates neither way; it still resolves that identity, still derives its key state, still admits it and grants to it, and what it records is that it read the head from one source.
- Two chains **diverge** when they share a prefix and each chain carries, at the first position after that prefix, an event the other chain does not carry. The **shared prefix** is the longest common prefix. A **divergent suffix** is the part of one chain after the shared prefix.
- Every key event's signed preimage binds the identifier, the event's sequence (which is its predecessor's plus one, above), the digest of its predecessor event, the event-type discriminator, the signer index list and the signature-form list of every signature group the kind carries, and every field a rule in this section reads (the revealed keys, the `standing_root` field, the installed root set and threshold, the key algorithm of every key and every commitment the event carries, the key-state snapshot, any key-event seal, the abandonment declaration, and the next commitment list and threshold, where the kind carries them). Every signature an event carries is over that one identical preimage. In the inception event the identifier field and the predecessor-digest field are the all-zero 32-byte placeholder (R13). Rationale: without predecessor binding, a party holding a root could copy the owner's genuine revealed `RootRecovery` bytes onto its own fork at the same sequence and present them as its own; without the index list in the preimage, a relay could strip one of several signatures with no detectable defect; without the form list in the preimage, a relay could rewrite one slot's declared form and re-split the whole signature field into a second self-consistent parse, which is the same stripping surface the index list closes; without the declaration in the preimage, a relay could strip the next commitment and substitute abandonment with every named signature still verifying.

**R1 — Closure: only a reveal moves the commitment.** The standing commitment MUST change only in a reveal-authorized event. A verifier MUST reject a chain in which the commitment changes at an event that reveals nothing. Rationale: if a root signature alone could fix a new commitment, an attacker holding the root would fix a commitment to keys the attacker holds, and the owner's later reveal would fail to match.

**R2 — Inception fixes the first commitment and the identifier is bound to the chain.** The inception event installs the first root set and threshold, fixes the first commitment list and next threshold, and carries the initial key state. Its root signature verifies against the root set the inception itself installs — the one event whose signers are named by the event they sign. A verifier MUST reject an inception event that fixes no commitment. A verifier MUST recompute the identifier from the inception event (R13) and MUST reject a chain whose recomputed identifier differs from the identifier under which the chain was served. The inception event also names each installed key's algorithm and the algorithm of each key its commitment list covers (definitions), and a verifier MUST reject an inception event carrying an algorithm value it does not recognize. **An identity is usable the moment its inception event is published**, because no rule of this section reads a cosignature: a newly incepted identity resolves, derives a key state, and ranks against any fork from its first event forward, whether or not any witness has yet cosigned it. Rationale: without the recomputation a relay could serve any self-consistent chain, including one whose inception is the attacker's own, under any identifier.

**R3 — Four event kinds; each kind fixes its signature set in a fixed layout; every named signature MUST verify.** A verifier MUST derive an event's kind from the type discriminator in its preimage, never from which signatures it carries, and MUST reject a chain containing an event whose discriminator it does not recognize (`Invalid{at_event}`). The kinds are `Inception`, `KeyState`, `CommitmentRollover`, and `RootRecovery`; these four names, and the condition names of §9.7.1, are the protocol's identifiers, and every SDK binding carries them unchanged as its variant names.

| Kind | Signatures the kind names | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `Inception` | a root signature by the installed root set | installs the first root; carries the initial key state; fixes the first commitment |
| `KeyState` | a root signature by the standing root | carries a complete key state and any key-event seals; changes no root and no commitment |
| `CommitmentRollover` | a reveal, and a root signature by the standing root | fixes the next commitment, or declares abandonment; changes no root and carries no key state |
| `RootRecovery` with `standing_root: CoSigns` | a reveal, a root signature by the installed set K′, and a root signature by the standing root | installs K′; carries the complete post-recovery key state; fixes the next commitment |
| `RootRecovery` with `standing_root: Lost` | a reveal, and a root signature by the installed set K′ | the same effect, without the standing root's signature |

**The signature set closes by construction.** Each kind — and for a `RootRecovery`, the kind together with its `standing_root` value — fixes which signature groups the event carries and in what order; within each group the preimage's signer index list fixes the slot count and the preimage's signature-form list fixes each slot's layout (definitions), so the signature field carries exactly one slot per named index, groups in the order the kind's table row lists them. **The field's length is a function of the kind, the index lists, the form lists, and the two length prefixes each assertion-form slot carries inside itself**, and the preimage binds every one of those but the last two, which sit inside the slot they measure and are bounded by `MAX_AUTHENTICATOR_DATA_BYTES` and `MAX_CLIENT_DATA_JSON_BYTES` (§9.18.17). A parser therefore walks the field slot by slot from the form list and lands exactly on its end. **Bytes past the last slot the form list accounts for are a copy-level defect, and so is a slot whose declared length prefix runs past the field or past its bound.** An event carries no signature its kind, index lists, and form lists do not name. Endorsements by third parties and cosignatures by witnesses are **separate signed objects over the event's preimage digest**, never carried on the event copy, and travel the channel §9.7.4.3 names. Rationale: a signature carried on the copy can be stripped or stuffed by any relay with no detectable defect, so evidence that rides the copy is neither reliably present nor reliably absent, and an unbounded signature field is a verification-cost amplifier; the index list in the preimage is what lets a threshold be checked over a set of more than t members while the layout stays closed.

A `RootRecovery` carries a required field `standing_root` with exactly two values, `CoSigns` and `Lost`, bound in the preimage. The value declares which set the event carries, so a `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` whose root signature is missing or fails to verify is a copy-level defect and never a `RootRecovery{Lost}`; the declaration in the preimage, not the presence of a signature, says which signatures the event names. **`Lost` is a controller obligation and no verifier checks it.** The signing quorum MUST establish that fewer than t members of the standing root are reachable before it declares `Lost`; where the SDK itself holds at least t members — the personal 1-of-1 case, and any case where one device holds the quorum — it MUST refuse, with a typed error, to sign `standing_root: Lost`. That refusal is the one mechanical realization of the obligation, and it cannot run for an organization whose five root members sit in five separate substrates, so every relying party reads `Lost` as the controller's declaration and never as a checked fact. **The `CoSigns` signature is what puts a recovery at rank 1 under R6's root rule**, so a controller that still holds a threshold of its root and declares `Lost` anyway gives up the rank its root would have bought it. A `RootRecovery` also carries the revealed keys, the installed root set K′ and its threshold with a root signature by K′ as proof of possession, the complete post-recovery key state (definitions), and the next commitment (R4); no revealed key is ever installed, and no root member is ever the preimage of any commitment, because K′ is generated fresh and the commitment names separate keys. A `CommitmentRollover` carries no key state: it changes the commitment, or declares abandonment, and nothing else.

**Every indexed signature MUST verify against the key at its index**: each revealed key's signature against the revealed public key at that index, each standing-root signature against the standing root member at that index, and each installed-root signature against the installed set's member at that index; the inception's root signatures verify against the set the inception installs. A signature that does not verify is absent. Two classes of invalidity follow. A **copy-level defect** — an indexed signature, at an index the set contains, that is absent or fails to verify; a corrupted byte; or extra bytes in the signature field — is producible by any relay with no key, so the verifier discards that copy, treats the position as unserved under R11 (`Inconclusive{CopyDefect}`), and never demotes the suffix or records anything against the identity; an intact copy from another source supersedes the mutilated one, and events are identified by preimage digest for that purpose. An **author-attributable defect** is signed content, so the verifier rejects the chain at that event (`Invalid{at_event}`, R14). The author-attributable defects are:

- a signer index list naming fewer than t root members, where **t is the threshold of the set that group indexes** — the standing root's own threshold for a standing-root group, and the installed set K′'s own threshold for an installed-root group, so a `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` carrying a 3-of-5 standing group and a 1-of-1 installed group satisfies this bullet on both; or a signer index list naming fewer than n next-set members, where n is the standing commitment's next threshold (definitions);
- a repeated index in any signer index list;
- a signer index that names no member of the set the group indexes — the index is bound in the preimage, so only the signer could have written it;
- a root set or a next set whose members are not pairwise distinct, or a commitment list whose entries are not pairwise distinct;
- a threshold t outside `1 ≤ t ≤ |root set|`, or a threshold n outside `1 ≤ n ≤ |commitment list|`;
- a sequence that is not its predecessor's plus one, or an inception event whose sequence is not zero (definitions);
- a revealed key whose commitment is not the standing list's member at the named index;
- **a `KeyState` whose snapshot installs a root set or a threshold differing from the standing root at its predecessor.** R3's effect column gives `KeyState` "changes no root and no commitment", R1 closes the commitment half, and this bullet closes the root half. Rationale: the definitions' load-bearing sentence — that the only two kinds installing a root are the inception event and a `RootRecovery` — is what R6's rank-criterion pin cites as its reason for reading tier at the revealing event's predecessor, and a rule the pin rests on is enforced here rather than asserted there;
- **a `CommitmentRollover` carrying a key state.** R3's effect column gives that kind "changes no root and carries no key state", and without this bullet two conforming verifiers reach different verdicts on one chain: one reads the effect column as a constraint and rejects, the other reads the defect list as exhaustive and accepts;
- a state-carrying event listing one public key more than once, whatever conditions the two entries carry;
- a key or a commitment carrying a key-algorithm value the verifier does not recognize (definitions);
- a signature-form list whose length differs from its group's signer index list, or one naming a form value the verifier does not recognize (definitions). Rationale: the form list is in the preimage, so only the signer could have written a value or a length it carries;
- an unrecognized discriminator;
- a next threshold n, fixed by the event, lower than the threshold of the root set standing **after** that event — the installed set K′'s threshold on a `RootRecovery`, and the unchanged standing root's threshold on every other kind, because the reveal that consumes this commitment will be checked against whichever root stands then. Rationale: a reveal-authorized event is as strong as the weaker of the two thresholds it stands between, so a 3-of-5 organization that fixed a 1-of-1 next set would be taken by whichever officer held that one pre-rotation key. Requiring n ≥ the standing root's threshold makes the recovery path as strong as the root it recovers, and a controller that deliberately installs a weaker root gets a next threshold measured against the root it actually installed;
- a state-carrying event whose service-key designation names a key that same snapshot does not list `current`, or names no key at all. A `RootRecovery` therefore installs at least one operational key, which R10's ceremony already generates. Rationale: the designation authenticates the service record (`03-identity.md` §3.10.13), so a designation naming a retired or absent key leaves the identity's transport metadata unverifiable until a later key event re-designates. The write decision reads no designation;
- a witnessing interval below `MIN_WITNESSING_INTERVAL` (§9.18.17). Rationale: an interval of zero makes R10's self-observation an unbounded fetch loop;
- **a witness set larger than `MAX_WITNESS_SET_SIZE` (§9.18.17), or one whose entries are not pairwise distinct.** Rationale: the witness set is a repeated field of the same preimage the key entries sit in, so an unbounded set puts an event past the `value` bound of one frame (§9.10.12) — and a reveal-authorized event that cannot be published has spent the standing commitment on nothing;
- a state-carrying event whose snapshot lists more than `MAX_KEYS_PER_CHAIN` keys (§9.18.17). Rationale: R8 makes every state-carrying event list every key the chain installed, and a `RootRecovery` installs a whole root set at once, so the resource a chain exhausts is its installed-key count and not its event count;
- **a state-carrying event whose snapshot is identical to its predecessor's** — one that installs no key, retires none, and changes no condition, designation, witness set, or cosigning parameter. Rationale: without it a root-threshold holder pads the chain with no-op `KeyState` events at a few hundred bytes each, and the padding costs the attacker almost nothing while it drives the chain toward a bound the owner's recovery then has to fit inside;
- **a witnessing interval above `MAX_WITNESSING_INTERVAL` (§9.18.17).** Rationale: the interval is the input to the freshness bound (definitions), so an unbounded interval makes a cosignature that never goes stale, which restores the no-freshness state the witness layer exists to remove;
- **a witness set naming the subject identifier itself.** Rationale: an identity that witnesses its own head supplies no independent observation, and a state-carrying event is the one place the protocol rejects that case mechanically;
- **a state-carrying event listing more than one key `current` in any single operational role.** Rationale: a role names one signer. Two keys `current` in `#active` give the identity two key-continuity fingerprints, so a peer comparing fingerprints reads a false MITM (§9.11); they let two service records verify at once (`03-identity.md` §3.10.13); and a second key slipped into the role keeps attesting after the controller rotates the first;
- **a state-carrying event that installs a key which appeared earlier on the same chain as a revealed next-set key.** Rationale: R4's key-uniqueness invariant counts installations, and a revealed pre-rotation key was never installed, so without this bullet a surviving Shamir share or paper copy of a spent pre-rotation key becomes the identity's live signer and no chain diverges to signal it;
- **an event fixing a commitment whose preimage is a key that same event installs.** Rationale: R4's commitment-freshness invariant reads the keys at or before the fixing event, and an event that commits to a key it also installs makes one compromise yield both the live root and the reveal that would recover from it;
- **a key state whose `delegator` field is nonzero, until ADR-064, the cooperative-delegation model, lands.** No section states how a verifier fetches the delegator's chain, finds the key-event seal anchoring this chain's establishment events, or ranks a delegated chain, so a verifier presented with a nonzero delegator has no rule to apply. The preamble of this section cites this bullet where it defers delegated precedence, and ADR-064 removes it;
- a violation of any R4 or R8 obligation.

**The criterion that separates the two classes** is whether a relay holding no key could have produced the defect: a defect a keyless relay could produce is copy-level, and a defect only the signer could have produced — because the preimage binds it — is author-attributable. The two lists above are the indicators a verifier applies; where a new defect fits neither list, the verifier decides it by that criterion. Authorization is the signature by the revealed private keys and never the reveal alone: a revealed public key is public the instant the event appears.

**R4 — Every reveal-authorized event fixes the next commitment or declares abandonment, under two closed invariants.** A reveal-authorized event either fixes the next commitment list and next threshold — the controller MUST generate those keys in a substrate satisfying §9.7.4.1 item 3a before it signs (R10 states when) — or carries an explicit **abandonment declaration** in place of a commitment and generates no next set. **Only a `CommitmentRollover` co-signed by the standing root may declare abandonment**: the party retiring an identity holds its root, and a controller whose root is lost recovers first (a `RootRecovery{Lost}` installs K′) and abandons under a second reveal co-signed by K′. A reveal alone cannot abandon. An event that fixes no commitment and declares no abandonment is invalid. **Abandonment is terminal at the position of the abandoning rollover, on the chain that carries it:** after a valid `CommitmentRollover` that declares abandonment, no further key event on that chain is valid, and a verifier MUST reject any event whose predecessor chain contains one. The predicate is validity under R1 through R4 on a chain the verifier adopted, and it is not the deleted `established` predicate a threshold of witness cosignatures used to supply: no cosignature enters any rule of this section, so a verifier that holds the abandoning rollover on the chain it adopted acts on it, and one that has adopted no chain carrying it has nothing to act on. The confirmation-before-social-action sentence below states what a relying party does before it acts on an adopted abandonment. The derived key state after abandonment is the state the latest state-carrying event before it carries, and the abandoning event changes nothing in it. **The abandoning controller owns the boundary:** before it publishes the abandoning rollover it MUST issue the MLS Update of §9.12 step 2 in every context it belongs to, and each of those Commits is the abandonment boundary in its context (§9.7.1). **In a context the controller cannot reach, there is no boundary, and §9.7.1 governs.** §9.7.1 states when a boundary for a key exists in a context, and an abandoning controller that never reached the context left nothing there for that test to find. The residual, stated plainly: that context's members keep verifying content under the abandoned identity's operational key until the controller reaches the context and issues the Update, and no member of it learns of the abandonment from the log alone. A controller that regains reach re-joins the context manually and issues the Update then (§9.12 states the step ordering). Content signed before abandonment verifies against the final state under §9.7.1; the abandoning Commit is the boundary for every key the final state lists `current`, so those keys verify nothing after it. **After a relying party confirms an abandonment, it MUST treat every UCAN the identity issued as revoked, every attestation it issued as expired, and every sender key it holds for the identity as retired**, and MAY then remove the identity from a context and withdraw its standing. **Confirmation before social action, for every adopted takeover and every abandonment:** a relying party MUST NOT act socially on a chain it adopted — remove an identity from a context, revoke its standing, treat it as abandoned — until it has confirmed that chain's head from a source the identity's service record does not name, because the holder of the designated key chose the relays that record names (definitions, fallback set). Two invariants hold over the whole chain, and a verifier MUST reject the event that breaks either: **key uniqueness over installation** — a public key is **installed** by at most one event on a chain and holds one role there (a root member or an operational key), and listing an already-installed key in a later state-carrying event's snapshot is not an installation, so a snapshot that re-lists every prior key breaks nothing; and **commitment freshness** — a fixed commitment equals no commitment fixed earlier on the chain, and equals the commitment of no key that appears on the chain at or before the fixing event, the keys that same event installs included. Rationale: a chain whose standing commitment has been consumed and neither replaced nor declared abandoned has no recovery path and no terminal state; a key that reappears or a commitment that repeats would let a spent or stolen key satisfy a later reveal; abandonment authorized by a reveal alone would let anyone who ever obtained enough of one next set kill a live identity whose root is intact; and a boundary nobody issues is a boundary nobody can read.

**R5 — The controller destroys the spent pre-rotation keys.** After the controller has retained the signed event (R10's retention invariant), the controller MUST destroy **every private key of the next set whose commitment list the reveal-authorized event superseded — every member, whether the reveal named it or not** — from every custody location and backup medium it controls, for a `RootRecovery` and a `CommitmentRollover` alike, and MUST NOT destroy them before that retention. A reveal that names n of a longer list leaves the unnamed members holding a commitment the shared prefix still carries, so a member left alive lets whoever later obtains it fork before the superseding event and reveal the same standing commitment; §9.7.4.1 item 8's retention obligation ends for the whole set at that same event, so no rule asks the controller to keep one. R5 reaches only the controller's own copies: a Shamir share held by a contact, a paper mnemonic already photographed, and a cloud key store's historical snapshot are outside its reach (§9.7.4.1 item 4's destructibility axis), so a controller using a non-destructible medium cannot fully comply and MUST be warned at custody selection. Rationale: a spent key that survives can join a second reveal of a commitment already consumed, which under R6 makes the identity contested at that fork.

**R6 — Fork precedence over valid suffixes: the root rule.** A verifier first evaluates validity (R1–R4) over each chain in full. A chain invalid at any event is `Invalid` and is not ranked. When a verifier holds two valid chains that diverge from a shared prefix, it MUST rank them as follows. Let C be the standing commitment at the shared prefix. On each divergent suffix, the verifier locates the first event that reveals C; at most one such event exists per suffix, because R1 makes C unique on the shared prefix, a reveal consumes it, and R4 forbids fixing it again. The verifier ranks each suffix:

1. the suffix carries an event that reveals C, **and that event also carries a root signature that verifies against the root standing at that event's predecessor**;
2. the suffix carries an event that reveals C, and that event carries no such root signature;
3. the suffix carries no event that reveals C.

A lower rank number beats a higher one, and the winning chain supersedes the losing chain in its entirety. **This is the root rule, and Alec settled it on 2026-09-07: "Root wins sounds like a good solution."**
