- *The rank-criterion pin — what rank reads, and where it reads it.* **Rank reads exactly two properties of a suffix: whether it carries an event revealing the shared prefix's standing commitment, and whether that event carries the signature of the root standing at its own predecessor. Rank reads nothing else.** The tier a root signature confers is read **at the revealing event's predecessor** and never at the event itself, and that position is what makes tier a property the shared prefix fixed rather than one the claimant chose. At the event itself the standing root of a `RootRecovery` is the fresh set K′ that the event installs, so every claimant would satisfy the test against its own key and the test would decide nothing. At the predecessor the standing root is the shared prefix's root, because the only two kinds that install a root are the inception event and a `RootRecovery`, a `RootRecovery` is itself reveal-authorized, and no reveal-authorized event sits below a suffix's *first* revealing event. **Alec's reason for the tier, verbatim: "If an attacker has the root, it's GG. What would you gain by trying to optimize against that case?"** A rule that ignored the root signature would be optimizing against an adversary who already holds the root, and it would cost the common case: a controller whose root is intact, facing a holder of a copied pre-rotation key, wins under this rule and would only tie without it.
- *The rank-monotonicity pin — rank is monotone in a suffix's own history.* Once a suffix carries a C-revealing event, its rank is fixed and does not change when the suffix is extended. A suffix with no C-revealing event is rank 3 until one appears (R7's pending tie). A reveal on a suffix that consumes a commitment the suffix itself fixed confers no rank, because the shared prefix never committed to those keys; without this clause a party whose recovery installed its own root and its own commitment could reveal that commitment on its own suffix and claim rank 1.

**Two suffixes at equal rank do not resolve: the identity is contested (R7), and no property of either event breaks the tie**, because over the properties rank reads the two suffixes are indistinguishable. A verifier MUST NOT decide a divergence by head sequence or by the first event after the shared prefix. When a verifier holds more than two chains, it ranks every pair against the standing commitment of that pair's own shared prefix; the winner is the chain that wins every pairwise comparison it is party to; the identity is contested when no such chain exists. **The composite tie takes the class of the strictest pair**: it is terminal by key material if any pair ties at rank 1 or at rank 2, and pending only where every tied pair ties at rank 3 (R7).

Rationale for the two comparisons this rule replaces: a comparison of the first event after the shared prefix lets the party that chooses the fork point choose which events are compared, so an attacker who forks among the owner's ordinary key events wins before the owner's recovery is read; a comparison of head sequences lets the party that appends the most events win.

**R7 — Ties and the contested verdict.** Equal rank resolves no winner, and the identity is **contested**. **The tie's class is a function of the tied rank number.** A tie at **rank 1** is terminal by key material and proves that two parties each held enough of the next set to reveal **and** a threshold of the shared prefix's root; that is the both-keys-compromised case, which §9.7.4.1's failure-mode table states is unrecoverable. A tie at **rank 2** is terminal by key material and proves that two parties each held enough of the next set while neither could produce the shared prefix's root signature; that is the lost-root-plus-copied-key case. **Ranks 1 and 2 are terminal for one reason, stated once: C is consumed on both suffixes, so no key event on either suffix changes the comparison.** A tie at **rank 3** is **pending** — neither suffix has revealed C. A later reveal on one suffix decides the divergence under R6. A later reveal on both decides it too where the two revealing events differ in whether they carry the shared prefix's root signature, because the rank numbers then differ; where they agree, the tie escalates to rank 1 or to rank 2 and becomes terminal. For a contested identity a verifier MUST return the `Contested` verdict (R14), which carries the tie class, the digests of every tied head, the digest of the shared prefix's head, the fork position, and R9's residue count; MUST NOT adopt any tied suffix as the **`current`** key state; and MUST retain every tied head and suffix in its retained set as evidence, where R9's bound and its rank-eviction rule fix what that set holds. The verifier uses the shared prefix's key state — the state both suffixes agree on, which the verdict's shared-prefix head identifies — as the verification basis for content a context placed before the fork; it verifies nothing after that, and it MUST NOT serve that state as `current`. The obligation is stated here so that R7 and §9.7.1 read the same, because two members that read a permission where one reads an obligation compute different governance outcomes from identical inputs and both conform. **"Before the fork" in a context anchors to the member's own observation:** content is before the fork when the member accepted it under an epoch no later than the last Commit that context observed from the identity **before the member first observed any divergent suffix of that identity**. A member that observed no such Commit — because the identity issued none in that context before the divergence reached it — verifies nothing for that identity in that context and reports `Invalid{no_prefork_basis}`, which is the fail-closed direction. Anchoring to the fork position instead would name a Commit that exists only when the fork position happened to retire a key, and three members would then read three different Commits for one message. Indicator, not criterion: two parties holding the key material is the usual cause of a terminal tie, and one controller publishing from two devices at one sequence is the usual cause of a pending one.

**A rank-3 tie has a cheap exit and the terminal ties have none.** A controller whose own two devices produced a rank-3 tie signs a `CommitmentRollover` or a `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` on the suffix it keeps (R10), which puts that suffix at rank 1 and decides the divergence at every party. **That exit spends the standing commitment**, so R10's avoidance is cheaper still: a controller's second device fetches the chain and re-signs its intended change on the head it finds, rather than publishing a second event at one sequence.

**No rule inside the log resolves a terminal tie, and this paragraph states why rather than leaving a reader to infer it.** Over the properties rank reads, the two revealing suffixes are indistinguishable: both parties held the pre-rotation keys, both events verify, and every further ordering rule a verifier could apply reads a property one of the two parties chose. A rule that ordered two valid reveals of one commitment by first observation would divide relying parties by what each happened to see first; a rule that ordered them by any field of the events would let the party that authors its event second read the first and match it. Every such rule also removes recovery from an unforeseen compromise, because the owner's own recovery is the second reveal in exactly the case the owner needs it. **Recorded so a later author does not rediscover it as a fix:** a double-authentication-preventing signature scheme, with the pre-rotation key as the DAPS key and the fork position as its address, would turn today's evidence into a compact portable proof that one commitment was consumed twice. It produces no winner, because extracting the leaked secret proves a double reveal happened and never which party is the controller, and no substrate §9.7.4.1 item 4 admits produces a DAPS signature. It is a candidate for the evidence layer alone and never for fork choice. When R6 later resolves a divergence that a verifier had recorded as a rank-3 tie, or the tie escalates to rank 1 or rank 2, the verifier MUST retire the rank-3 equivocation record and MUST NOT count it against the identity. §9.11's clearing rule reads that retirement, and §9.11 is where the rule that clears a standing is stated. Rationale for refusing the shared prefix's state as current: if the root was compromised before the fork point, the shared prefix already carries the attacker's events.

**R8 — Key state is the latest state-carrying event; nothing is voided; nothing is omitted.** **A relying party derives the key state from the latest state-carrying event at or before the head of the chain it adopted.** The derived key state at any position IS the key state the latest state-carrying event at or before that position carries; no earlier event contributes, and a verifier applies no diff, no undo, and no voiding. A `RootRecovery` therefore replaces the entire key state in one event: every operational key an attacker installed, every condition an attacker asserted, every witness-set and service-key change an attacker made, is absent from the recovery's **roles** and so is not `current` after it. **Every key the chain installed appears in the snapshot with a condition.** Every key that any event at or before the snapshot's own position installed — as a root member or as an operational key, whoever authored the installing event — MUST appear in the snapshot in exactly one of the four conditions of §9.7.1, and a state-carrying event that omits one is malformed: a verifier MUST reject it (`Invalid{at_event}`). A key the recovering controller believes an attacker installed is listed `Compromised{from: N}`, never left out. Each key appears **once**; a snapshot that lists one public key twice, under two conditions or under the same one, is malformed on the same terms. Rationale: nothing is voided and nothing is omitted, so a compromise is asserted and never implied by silence — a controller that could drop a key by leaving it out would repudiate every signature that key ever made, which is the outcome §9.7.1 promises cannot happen, and a verifier reading a snapshot that lists one key twice would resolve the same key to two conditions. The recovery's operational-key roles replace the derived roles in full: an operational role the snapshot does not name is not `current` after the recovery. **The same replacement governs every other key-state field**, so a `KeyState` changes the witness set, the witnessing interval, each key's algorithm, and the service-key designation by carrying new values for them, and R3 bounds the witnessing interval wherever an event carries it. A `Compromised{from: N}` entry carries a position in this log, bounded N ≤ the sequence of the event that carries the entry; a verifier MUST reject an entry whose N exceeds that. **N has exactly one reader outside this section**: the log-anchored evidence class of §9.7.1, which reads N in the test that section states for an artifact signed under a key the state lists `Compromised`. No other content rule reads N — §9.7.1's content class orders content by the context epoch and never by N — and no rule in this section rejects a controller's event on account of content. A `CommitmentRollover` carries no key state and changes the derived state in no way; the derived state after an adopted rollover is the state of the latest state-carrying event before it, which after a divergence may be an event on the shared prefix. Chain validity under R1–R4 is evaluated over the full chain with every event present. Rationale: an attacker holding the root who appends `KeyState` events to the one genuine log produces no divergence, so R6 never runs, and only a full replacement of the roles removes what it installed; a rule that voided by boundary needed an expanding list of exemptions; and a controller that could omit any key by silence could repudiate its own history, which the listing obligation forbids for every key the chain installed. A state-carrying event costs O(keys) bytes, a few kilobytes on a log whose key events are rare.

**R9 — Retention of evidence, the store, and the retention bound.** A verifier MUST NOT bound the suffix it retains at its last reveal-authorized event, because a party holding a spent pre-rotation key can anchor a fork before that event (R5).

**The store, and what a slot holds.** A validating relay keeps one slot per **(routing id, divergent suffix)**. Two chains that are prefix-related are one chain for this purpose and collapse into one slot, which holds the longer of them; two chains that diverge (definitions) occupy two slots, whether or not their heads stand under the same root set. Keying the slot on the root set alone would put every divergence that has not yet installed a new root into one slot, and the write rule would then hand that slot to whichever party published first — the owner's own two devices in a rank-2 tie, or a root thief who forked at an ordinary `KeyState` — so no verifier would ever hold both chains and R6 would never run. **A slot holds the full chain from the inception event to its head**, not a head alone, and a relay serving a head serves every predecessor of that head, because R6 ranks chains and a slot that held only heads would leave a verifier unable to rank the head it was served. **The relay's slot set is the same structure this rule tells a verifier to keep** — the bound and the eviction rule below govern both — so a relay and a verifier hold the same evidence under one rule. `03-identity.md` §3.10.2 states the relay's validation procedure and applies this rule without restating it, and §9.10.12 states the frame's bytes and cites §3.10.2 for the procedure.

**The write rule reads the assembled chain, and a slot is an ordered sequence of frames.** A relay stores, per routing id, the events every accepted frame carried, and each slot is one maximal chain through them. A relay accepts a frame on one of two preconditions, which are the two branches §3.10.2 step 3 verifies:

- **the frame's first event is an inception event** that recomputes to the routing id's identifier — the frame is verified from that event under R2 and R3, with the identifier recomputation, and it either opens the routing id's first slot or, where a slot already holds an inception event with the same §9.5.1 preimage digest, takes the idempotent-refresh outcome below;
- **the frame's first event's predecessor digest names an event the relay already holds at that routing id** — the frame is verified under R2 and R3 against the standing root the assembled chain carries at the named position, with no recomputation.

A relay rejects a frame that meets neither precondition, and the publisher's remedy is to publish the intervening segments first, in order.

**An event is identified by its §9.5.1 preimage digest, and every comparison below reads that digest and never the bytes.** Two events whose preimage digests are equal are one event whatever their signature bytes, and two chains whose events have pairwise equal preimage digests are one chain. **A relay or a verifier that holds a valid event and receives a byte-different valid event with the same preimage digest keeps the copy it holds and discards the arriving one**, so the two never occupy two slots and never rank as two suffixes. Rationale, stated because the alternative reading freezes an honest identity: §9.5 admits a hardware signer that draws its own nonce, and the SDK's default root and pre-rotation custody is a passkey, so one preimage under one key yields many verifying signatures. R10's own resume paragraph produces a second such encoding, and a copy of the first escapes through a lost acknowledgement, a swap file, or a device backup. Under a byte comparison those two encodings of one reveal-authorized event would be two suffixes revealing one commitment, both rank 1 under R6, and R7 would contest the identity terminally on a duplicate of its own signature.

**The outcomes are stated over the first differing position, and the enumeration is exhaustive by construction.** For an accepted frame the relay compares the preimage digest of each of the frame's events, in order, against the preimage digest of the event the slot already holds at that position. Exactly one of three cases applies:

1. **Every event of the frame has the same preimage digest as the event the slot holds at its position, and the frame's range lies inside the slot's chain.** The frame is an idempotent TTL refresh and changes no slot. This is the case a 6-day republication of an inception-only chain takes.
2. **Every event of the frame that the slot already holds has the same preimage digest as the stored one, and the frame continues past the slot's head.** The frame's remaining events extend that slot and the slot's chain grows by them. A publisher whose chain outgrew one frame publishes segment two this way (`03-identity.md` §3.10.5 step 3), and a healing frame that overlaps a slot and extends past it takes this case too.
3. **A first position exists where the frame's event has a preimage digest the event the slot holds there does not have.** The two chains diverge (definitions) at that position, so the frame's events form a second divergent suffix over the shared prefix, and that suffix occupies its own slot under the admission and eviction rule below. The relay stores the prefix once and both suffixes over it. A relay replaces no event it already holds, and a re-encoding of an event it already holds takes case 1 or case 2 rather than this one.

**Serving order and the page walk.** A relay serves a slot's frames in ascending order of each segment's first sequence, and stores each frame with a `stored_at` preserving that order. **The walk is over a routing id and not over one slot**, because ADR-004's QUERY filters by `stored_at` per routing id and carries no slot selector: a resolver re-issues QUERY with `since` set to the `stored_at` of the last frame it accepted **at that routing id**, takes every frame each page returns, and assembles the frames into slots itself by predecessor digest. It stops when every slot it has opened runs from inception to a head, or when a page returns no frame it does not already hold. A resolver that advanced `since` per slot would skip the frames of a second slot interleaved below its own high-water mark, and the termination condition would never bring them back. This sentence is the one home of the page walk; §9.10.12 and `03-identity.md` §3.10.2 cite it, and no rule reads a wire parameter ADR-004's QUERY does not carry.

**The retained set holds two further object kinds, and both stay bounded by construction.** A verifier retains, per retained suffix, at most one cosigned head per witness the key state at that suffix's head names — the highest sequence it verified from that witness on that suffix — and **at most one conflict statement per (witness, retained suffix) pair**, keeping the one whose `observed_at` is highest. **Both keys are (witness, retained suffix)**, so `MAX_WITNESS_SET_SIZE` × `MAX_RETAINED_SUFFIXES` (§9.18.17) bounds each at 256 objects per identity, which is 61,440 bytes of cosigned heads and 63,488 bytes of conflict statements at the fixed widths §9.7.4.3 states, and this rule needs no constant of its own. **A key of (witness, offered digest) would need one**: a conflict statement's `offered_digest` is the digest of the offered chain's event at the sequence where it first differs (§9.7.4.3) and not a head digest, and a party holding a threshold of the subject's root offers one witness any number of distinct divergent chains, so the count of distinct offered digests is bounded by that party's publication rate and by nothing in this corpus. **Both kinds are evidence a party may read and never an input to a verdict** (definitions), so a verifier that retains none of them reaches every verdict of this section unchanged.

**Why a flood of slots takes no identity.** Every event a slot holds stands under a root the relay verified from the inception event forward, so a party that opens many slots has signed every one of them under a root it holds. Slot count confers nothing: R6 ranks a suffix by whether it reveals the shared prefix's standing commitment and by whose root signed that reveal, and neither property gets easier to satisfy by publishing more forks. A party that cannot produce a reveal fills the retained set with rank-3 suffixes, which the eviction rule below discards first; a party that can produce one needs exactly one suffix to reach the rank it is entitled to. A flood of slots is therefore a symptom of an already-taken root, never a way to take an intact identity.

**A chain is served in segments, and the installed keys have a ceiling.** A frame carries a **contiguous segment** of one chain: its `value` names the segment's first and last sequence, and the events between them in order. A relay serves a slot's chain as an ordered sequence of frames covering inception through head, and a resolver assembles the segments and verifies from the inception event, or from a state-carrying event on that chain it already holds. **A chain installs at most `MAX_KEYS_PER_CHAIN` keys (§9.18.17), and R3 rejects a state-carrying event whose snapshot exceeds that count.** The ceiling counts installed keys and not events, because R8 makes every state-carrying event list every key the chain ever installed, so a chain's bytes grow with the square of its installed-key count and the count is what the `value` bound of one frame constrains. **A sequence carries no ceiling of its own**, so a `CommitmentRollover` — which installs nothing — and any event on a chain below the key ceiling are admissible at any sequence. **An event that installs no key consumes no ceiling budget**, which is why a party that appends no-op state-carrying events buys itself nothing here; R3's identical-snapshot bullet rejects such an event outright. **A verifier that meets an event past the ceiling keeps the chain up to it and rejects that event and its successors** (`Invalid{at_event}` at the first event past the ceiling), so it holds a ranked chain rather than nothing, and a controller learns the bound at the event it signs rather than discovering it as an unpublishable frame. **One event MUST still fit one frame**, and §9.18.17 states the arithmetic that keeps the largest event on a full chain, its witness set included, inside the `value` bound of §9.10.12. Snapshot entries themselves stay complete: segmentation splits a chain across frames and omits no event and no entry, so R8's listing obligation is untouched.

**The retention bound and its eviction rule.** A verifier retains at most `MAX_RETAINED_SUFFIXES` divergent suffixes per identity, and at most `MAX_RETAINED_SUFFIX_BYTES` of suffix bytes per routing id (§9.18.17). **The eviction rank is a scalar, and it is the R6 rank the suffix carries against the chain it forked from**, read against the commitment that stands, at that suffix's own fork position, on that chain: 1 where the suffix carries an event revealing that commitment and that event also carries the root standing at its own predecessor, 2 where the suffix reveals that commitment without such a root signature, and 3 where the suffix reveals nothing. Eviction reads that one term and nothing else; R6's pairwise comparison decides adoption and no eviction reads it. A rank-3 suffix is the cheap thing to manufacture, which is why the scalar puts it last. A candidate that would exceed the count bound is **admitted**, and the verifier evicts the retained suffix carrying the **highest rank number**, breaking a tie among equal ranks by evicting the suffix it observed **most recently**. **The tie-break direction is load-bearing and the opposite direction defeats the rule's own claim:** a party holding a threshold of the subject's root, and no reveal, publishes `MAX_RETAINED_SUFFIXES` rank-3 forks against an owner whose live chain is also rank 3 until it reveals, and evicting the earliest-observed equal-rank suffix would evict the owner's chain first at every verifier and every validating relay that had been following the identity. Evicting the most recently observed puts the flood's own members at the front of the queue instead, which is what makes the paragraph above's claim — that a flood is a symptom of an already-taken root and never a way to take an intact identity — true of the retained set and not of rank alone. **A rank-1 suffix is never evicted.** When every retained suffix is rank 1 and another rank-1 candidate arrives, the verifier keeps the retained set and returns `Contested{…, residue: n}`, where n counts the rank-1 candidates it could not retain — the identity is contested either way, and the residue tells the consumer that more tied suffixes exist than the verdict enumerates. **A validating relay in that state rejects the further rank-1 frame and records its head digest**, and rule (a) of §3.10.2 names that rejection as a conforming outcome; a relay adopts no chain, so it returns no verdict. A routing id whose retained bytes reach `MAX_RETAINED_SUFFIX_BYTES` likewise serves what it holds, accepts no further suffix, and reports the same residue. A resolver MAY stop fetching once it holds two rank-1 suffixes, because R7 makes the verdict terminal there and further bytes change nothing it can decide. Each eviction records the evicted head event's preimage digest so that a re-served copy — including one carrying a different encoding of the same signatures — is recognized as one the verifier already ranked. R7's obligation to retain every tied head, and R14's persistence obligation, are scoped to the retained set. Rationale for the count: two devices plus several attacker forks fit with margin. Rationale for admitting rather than refusing: a bound with no eviction rule makes a flood the winning move, because a party that publishes eight rank-2 forks first would fill every slot before the owner's rank-1 recovery arrived and the recovery would never be ranked. Indicator, not criterion: key-event logs are short, so a verifier that retains in full every chain it holds satisfies R11's evidence obligation with no fetch.

**R10 — The controller's procedure.**

*Which event to sign.* No ordering exists between the events; the controller signs the one row that matches its situation:

| The controller… | signs |
|---|---|
| holds a threshold of its root and must retire it — because any root-signed event on its chain exists that it did not author, fork or not; because it asserts a compromise; or for any reason it must move the root, including a custody substrate being decommissioned | `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` |
| holds a threshold of its root, trusts it, and suspects only a pre-rotation key copied | `CommitmentRollover` |
| has lost its root | `RootRecovery{Lost}` |
| is retiring the identity permanently and holds its root | `CommitmentRollover` declaring abandonment (R4), after the §9.12 step-2 Update in every context |
| finds its identity contested and wishes to retire it | `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` on its own suffix first, retiring the contested root set, then abandonment under K′. The order forecloses a copy holder's extension past the commitment K′ fixes and ends no contest (§9.7.4.1's failure-mode table, the two-phase row) |
| finds its designated witnesses unreachable, silent, or refusing, and wants its peers corroborating its head from a witness read rather than from their own two-relay reads | a `KeyState` naming a fresh witness set. No cosignature gates that event, so the replacement takes effect the moment relying parties adopt it; §9.7.4.3 states how the fresh witnesses reach a baseline. **The identity keeps working while its witnesses are silent**, because a peer corroborates its head from two relays instead (definitions) |

A reveal-authorized event puts its suffix at rank 1 or rank 2 against any divergence whose shared prefix's standing commitment it reveals — rank 1 where the event carries the root standing at its predecessor, rank 2 where it does not (R6, the rank-monotonicity pin) — so a controller that still holds a threshold of its root buys a rank by signing `CoSigns` rather than `Lost`. The choice among rows is otherwise a choice of what the event does to the key state. The SDK MUST treat any reveal-authorized event on its identity's chain that it did not sign, and any root-signed event it did not author, as evidence of compromise and alert at maximum severity; it MUST be able to extend a suffix the controller authored even after its own verifier adopted or contested a competing chain — the SDK extends its own suffix as the controller and, as a relying party on its own identity's state, holds the `Contested` verdict like any other party; and when its own identity is contested it MUST tell the human the tie class R7's verdict carries, because a rank-3 tie has the exit R7 states and a terminal tie has none. **A controller's device that is about to publish an event at a sequence the chain already carries a different event at MUST discard its own event, fetch the chain, and re-sign its intended change on top of the head it finds — where its own event is not reveal-authorized.** Because a `KeyState` carries a complete key state (R8), re-signing costs one event and loses nothing, and the obligation is what keeps two devices racing at one sequence from producing the rank-3 tie whose only other exit spends a commitment (R7). **Where the controller's own pending event is reveal-authorized, the obligation does not apply and the controller MUST NOT discard it.** That event has already consumed the standing commitment, which is the boundary the paragraph below states abort respects; a controller that discarded it and re-signed its intended change would reveal that commitment a second time, put two equally-ranked revealing suffixes on the chain, and contest its own identity permanently under R7. The controller instead keeps the signed event, completes its ceremony from the recovery handle, and extends its own suffix under the table above; it recomposes nothing and it signs no second reveal. A controller SHOULD roll over on suspicion, not on a schedule: every rollover mints a spent key whose later leak contests the identity, so a schedule multiplies that exposure with no threat-driven gain.

*Self-observation.* The SDK MUST fetch its own identity's log at a stated cadence — no less often than the witnessing interval the key state names, and on every launch — from at least one relay in the fallback set (definitions), and MUST treat an inability to complete that check as an alert, not as silence. **On its own chain the controller reads corroboration as every relying party does** (definitions): it fetches its own cosigned heads from the `scp:wit:` address §9.7.4.3 names, and it reads its own head back from two fallback-set entries under distinct declared operators. **It reports what it found and withholds nothing on it**, because no rule reads corroboration (definitions). Where the witness source alone is missing, the SDK reports a degraded witness posture, and the remedy is the witness-set replacement of the table above, a `KeyState` that spends no commitment. Where it holds neither source, the SDK reports that too, and it does not alert at compromise severity: a controller alerting at that severity on its own relay reachability would raise an alarm about a state no peer of it treats as one. The controller changes the witness set and the witnessing interval by a `KeyState` (R8), so neither value is fixed for the life of the identity.

*Before composing a snapshot.* The controller MUST hold its chain from the inception event, obtained from at least one relay in the fallback set (definitions); MUST enumerate **every key any event at or before the snapshot's position installed**, with each key's custody type and the condition the snapshot will give it, so that the snapshot lists each one exactly once (R8); MUST diff the chain's current witness set and service-key designation against the inception's or against a pre-compromise record it holds and surface every entry introduced at or after the asserted N; and MUST diff its service record's relay list against a pre-compromise copy it holds, because an attacker holding the designated key rewrites that list without touching the log (§9.6.3). **The refusal splits by verdict.** On `Inconclusive` the controller MUST refuse to sign, with a typed error, because it cannot see the events whose keys R8 requires it to enumerate. On `Contested` the controller refuses only when it cannot identify which suffix it authored; when it can, it composes over that suffix and signs, which is what the paragraph below tells the SDK to keep doing. A blanket refusal on `Contested` would lock the controller out of the first row of the table above, whose whole premise is a root-signed event some other party wrote onto the chain.

*Device boundary.* A reveal-authorized event MUST be produced on a device, and its successor keys — K′, the operational keys the snapshot installs, and the next set — generated in custody, that the controller has established the asserted compromise did not reach. **What the boundary has to separate is the successor keys and the assembling device from the compromise, and the means differ by where the root and the pre-rotation credentials reside.**

**For exportable custody** — a key the controller can move, which on the consumer profile is no root or pre-rotation key and on the headless profile is the three copyable methods of §9.7.4.1 item 4 — the boundary is a fresh platform account: when the compromise reached a platform account (a cloud-keystore account, a device-management enrolment, a restore image), the controller MUST create a fresh platform account and MUST NOT restore any state from the compromised one before proceeding.

**For a non-exportable root — a passkey, a FIDO2 token, an HSM — a fresh platform account is not the boundary and cannot be**, because the credential the recovery signs with does not move to it and the row's own theft-resistance argument is that no export path exists. **The recovery signs where the root and pre-rotation credentials reside**, and the boundary the controller establishes is a **fresh operational keychain on a device the compromise did not reach**: the successor set, K′, and the `#active` key the snapshot installs are generated there, and the assembling device is that device. A controller that asserts a compromise of the platform account holding a passkey therefore signs from that account and installs successors that account cannot reach, rather than abandoning the account and finding itself unable to reveal at all. A FIDO2 token and an HSM principal are not the platform account either, so the same reading covers them.

A controller that cannot establish the boundary by whichever of the two means its custody names MUST NOT proceed. The controller MUST confirm, before the reveal signs, that the event carries **the commitment list of the keys it just backed up, the next threshold that list carries, the K′ set and the root threshold it installs, and whether the event declares abandonment** — every field whose substitution changes who controls the identity afterwards — on a device with a display that is not the assembling device, or by comparing a displayed digest of the event against one the pre-rotation substrate computes. **What that confirmation establishes, and what it does not.** It binds the bytes the signing substrate consumes to the event the human read, which closes substitution between the assembling device and the token, because a hardware token with no display signs whatever it is shown. It establishes nothing about where the committed successor keys came from: the human is reading values the assembling device produced, so a compromised assembler that generated K′ and the next set itself passes the check. The provenance of those keys is the device boundary of this paragraph, and nothing else. The recovery entry point names the fresh custody as a required input with no default and no fallback to the daily provider.

*Ceremony order.* Enumerate (above); for an event that fixes a commitment, generate the next set under §9.7.4.1 items 1, 3a, 4, and 5b–5d — an abandoning event generates none; for a `RootRecovery`, generate K′ and the operational keys under the device boundary; for an abandoning rollover, issue the §9.12 step-2 Update in every context (R4); **persist the recovery handle carrying the composed preimage (below); sign** — each pre-rotation key signs where it resides and is never imported into operational custody; publish; retain; destroy. **The persist precedes the signature**, so no window exists in which a signed reveal exists that no handle names: a controller that crashed between signing and persisting would resume by composing a fresh next set, and its second event would reveal the standing commitment a second time and contest its own identity permanently. Because a software signer draws its nonce deterministically under RFC 6979 (§9.5), a resume that signs the persisted preimage produces the byte-identical event the first attempt would have produced, so a resume mints no second reveal. **A hardware signer that draws a random nonce produces a different signature over the same preimage**, and the resume is still safe, because the reveal that consumed the commitment is the one the handle names and the resume publishes exactly that persisted event rather than signing a second one; where the handle's phase is the pre-signature one, a second signature over the same preimage is a second encoding of one reveal and never a second reveal. **Retention is the invariant that makes destruction safe:** the controller MUST durably retain the signed event it published, byte for byte, as the head of its own chain BEFORE it destroys the spent keys (R5). Retaining those exact bytes is what lets the controller re-publish the copy it sent; a second encoding of the same preimage would be the same event at every reader (R9), so retaining exact bytes is a convenience of republication and not a condition of identity. **Confirmed publication** is durable self-retention together with acceptance of the event by at least one relay in the fallback set (definitions), and the controller MAY publish to any additional relay at any time. **The recovery replaces no service record**: the record is a separate object under a separate key, so a controller whose recovery designates a new service key MUST write a fresh service record under that key before its relay list is authenticated again (§9.6.3). **The ceremony's fallback set excludes nothing the identity's service record names** (definitions), so no holder of the designated key can empty it. **The SDK MUST refuse, with a typed error, to compose a reveal-authorized event when it can reach no source in that set**, because a controller that cannot read its own chain from a source an attacker did not choose cannot establish what it is recovering from, and a ceremony that could never reach confirmed publication would consume the standing commitment for nothing. **The ceremony completes at confirmed publication plus destruction, and witness submission gates neither.** The controller destroys the spent keys once it has durably retained the signed event, because holding them longer would enlarge exactly the exposure R5 exists to close and because keeping them changes nothing a verifier reads: the event's bytes are already fixed and no cosignature enters any rule of this section. **The controller submits the event to the witness set its snapshot names and resumes that submission from the handle's last phase**, so that its own later heads carry cosigned evidence a peer can read for freshness without a two-relay read of its own (definitions).

*The recovery handle.* The controller MUST durably persist a typed recovery handle BEFORE it signs the event, and phase 1 begins at that persist. The handle is a tagged union over two axes — the phase (composed and persisted but not yet signed; signed but no publish sent; a publish sent, acknowledged or not; published and confirmed but the spent keys not yet destroyed; submitted to the witness set with no cosigned head yet returned, which the resume continues by re-submitting and re-signs nothing) and the event's kind of continuation (`FixesCommitment` or `Abandons`) — and each variant carries only what its resume consumes: the composed preimage in every phase, the exact signed bytes the ceremony produced from the second phase on, the spent keys' custody handles in every phase before destruction, and, in a `FixesCommitment` variant only, the next set's custody handles and the expected commitment list so a resume re-checks it; the `Abandons` variant has no next-set field by construction rather than by absence. Exactly one named accessor loads a pending handle after a restart, and exactly one resume entry point consumes the handle by value and continues from its phase — signing the persisted preimage in the first phase, and re-signing nothing in any later phase.

*Abort.* **Abort is available in phase 1 alone — composed and persisted, not yet signed — and in no phase after the signature.** In phase 1 it consumes the handle by value and MUST destroy the composed preimage and the next set the ceremony generated, and it leaves the standing commitment unconsumed and the chain unchanged, which is the guarantee abort carries and the only place that guarantee is true. From the signature onward the controller resumes from the handle and recomposes nothing.

**The boundary abort respects is the SIGNATURE, not the publish.** R3's own criterion is that the signature is the authorizing act, so a signed reveal-authorized event has consumed the standing commitment whether or not it left the device. A controller that destroyed a signed event believing the commitment unconsumed would later reveal that commitment a second time and contest its own identity permanently under R7, and the surviving copy needs only to reach one relay: a lost acknowledgement, an operating system's swap file, or a device backup is enough. **The remedy for a wrong device-boundary establishment discovered after signing** is not abort: the controller publishes the signed event E1, then signs a further reveal-authorized event under the commitment E1 fixed, generated inside the boundary it meant to establish. That path costs one extra event on the chain and consumes one next set; abort at that point would cost the identity. From the signed state the controller's paths are: complete the ceremony from the handle, or extend its suffix under the table above. Never abort.

**R11 — Divergence is a positive finding.** A candidate head is **on the accepted chain** when either chain is a prefix of the other. A candidate head is **divergent** when the verifier holds both chains back to a common ancestor and they diverge (definitions). A candidate that is neither — because the events linking it to the accepted chain were not served, or because a served copy failed verification as a copy-level defect (R3) — is **inconclusive**: the verifier holds its baseline, records nothing, and returns the `Inconclusive` verdict (R14), which names the candidate head, the accepted head, the missing sequence range, and every source that failed to serve the link; before returning it the verifier MUST have tried at least one relay in the fallback set (definitions). R9's retention bound never produces this verdict, because R9 admits the candidate and evicts by rank instead.

**First contact takes two relays under distinct declared operators.** A verifier holding no accepted baseline for an identifier MUST obtain that identifier's chain from at least two entries of the community relay list **under distinct declared operators** (definitions). Only a community-list entry declares an operator, so a relay outside that list counts toward neither number, and two entries the list declares under one operator identity are one source the verifier counts once. That verifier holds no accepted service record either, so the definitions make the whole community relay list its fallback set and both sources are fallback-set entries; a fallback set carrying fewer than two entries under distinct declared operators cannot meet this floor. **A declared operator counts only where the relay serves a relay proof of control the verifier checks** — the object §9.7.4.3 defines, whose five checks that section states, whose `nonce` the verifier supplied on its own QUERY, and whose signing operator's chain the verifier resolves under the non-recursive floor of the definitions above (`18-addressability-and-deployment.md` §18.5.1 states the obligation an entry carries, states why a floor read off an unproven operator field is delivered against nobody, and states that the shipped list's entries are established as independent parties at release). A relay that serves no such proof, or one whose proof fails any of the five checks, counts as one unattributed source and can never be the second of two. **It settles the chains those relays served under R6, R7, and R12, never by sequence:** it compares the two chains event by event over their preimage digests (R9), so two encodings of one chain are one chain; where the two are prefix-related, the longer extends the shorter and the two are one chain (R12); where they diverge, the verifier ranks them (R6) and reports a tie as `Contested` (R7). Comparing head sequences across the sources would hand a root thief every first contact, because the thief appends events at will and R6 exists to refuse exactly that comparison. **Every degree below the floor is fail-closed: the verdict is `Inconclusive{SingleSource}` (R14) and the verifier adopts no head.** The degrees are reaching no second relay, reaching two relays that prove one operator identity between them, and holding a fallback set with fewer than two entries under distinct declared operators. The SDK reports the cause rather than a generic failure, so the human sees that the identity was not resolved rather than that it does not exist. Rationale: a single relay can always serve a genuine prefix truncated before the event the reader most needs — the `KeyState` that retires a stolen key, or the recovery that replaced a root. Such a prefix forks nothing, so R6 never runs; it verifies clean under R2 and R3; and a first-contact verifier holds no baseline, so R12 has nothing to compare. No relay-side gate detects it either, because such a gate is code an untrusted operator omits. Two relays are what a first-contact reader has instead, and a later reader holding a baseline has R12 and R6. **A fresher head does not relax this floor**, and that relaxation is rejected here so a later author does not rediscover it as a saving. A verifier MUST record equivocation evidence only from two chains it holds in full back to their common ancestor, and only when R6 resolves them as a tie (R7). Rationale: a single relay that withholds linking events, or serves a mutilated copy, could otherwise cause a verifier to record a durable verdict against an honest identity.

**R12 — Sequence governs the accepted chain only.** For a candidate head on the accepted chain, a verifier **discards** a head at a sequence strictly lower than the highest it has accepted, changing no stored state and returning `Discarded{accepted_head}` (R14), and an equal sequence with an identical head confirms the baseline (`Confirmed`). A discarded stale candidate is not a resolution outcome: the verifier stays on the baseline it already held, so a consumer that treated a discard as a completed resolution would run its next check against a baseline the candidate did nothing to refresh. A candidate whose chain is neither a prefix of the accepted chain nor extended by it is classified under R11 and decided under R6 and R7. A divergent candidate's head sequence decides nothing. **The stored baseline is the head of the chain the verifier adopted.** Adopting an R6 winner MAY lower the accepted head sequence (`Adopted{baseline_decreased}`), and a verifier's stored baseline MUST be able to express that decrease. Rationale: a monotone maximum cannot record the adoption of a lower-sequence winner.

**R13 — Domain separators and the identifier's construction.** The key-event signature preimage uses `"SCP-KEL-EVENT-V1:"` under the §9.5.1 construction, and the first field after the separator is the event-type discriminator byte, because a verifier derives the kind before it parses the fields the kind carries. The pre-rotation commitment uses `"SCP-PREROTATION-COMMITMENT-V1:"` as a commitment-hash domain, and the key-event seal uses `"SCP-KEL-SEAL-V1:"`. The witness layer's two objects use `"SCP-COSIGNED-HEAD-V1:"` and `"SCP-WITNESS-CONFLICT-V1:"`, whose preimages §9.7.4.3 constructs. The **identifier** is `SHA-256("SCP-KEL-ID-V1:" || inception_signed_preimage)`, where `inception_signed_preimage` is the inception event's complete signed preimage with its identifier and predecessor-digest fields set to the all-zero placeholder (definitions); a verifier recomputes it under R2. The identifier separator is distinct from the event separator so that no signature preimage's digest is ever an identifier and no identifier is ever a digest a key signed. All four separators are registered in §9.18.2.

**The routing derivation over an identifier is `routing_id = SHA-256("scp:did:" || identifier_bytes)`** over the identifier's 32 raw digest bytes. A relay checks a frame's `identifier` field against the routing id it was published at under that derivation (§9.10.12), and §3.10.2 cites this sentence rather than restating it. The witness layer adds two further derivations over the same identifier bytes, `SHA-256("scp:wit:" || identifier_bytes)` for cosigned heads and `SHA-256("scp:wcf:" || identifier_bytes)` for conflict statements, so each object kind is addressed at its own domain and the address is the type discriminant (§9.10.12). The derivation consumes the digest and never a textual encoding, so it is fixed now and waits on nothing; the identifier's textual form is a display and URL concern, and a later revision of this section fixes it together with the preimage field order. Because the identifier encodes no key, the routing id derives no key either. The root is a key list with a threshold from the first version, so that an organization and a person incept under one schema; the inception preimage's shape is frozen by the identifier construction, and a shape absent at inception cannot be added to an existing identity.

**R14 — The verdict taxonomy.** A resolution returns exactly one of the following, and every rule above cites its variant; every SDK binding carries the six names unchanged. A resolution either delivers a verified key state at or newer than the accepted head, or it does not; `Invalid` and `Contested` reject on their own terms. `Confirmed` — the candidate is the accepted head (R12). `Adopted{head, baseline_decreased}` — the verifier adopted a new head, on the accepted chain or by R6, and the flag says whether the accepted sequence decreased (R12). `Contested{tie: TerminalByKeyMaterial | Pending, heads, shared_prefix_head, fork_position, residue}` — equal rank under R6, with the tie class R7 derives from the tied rank number, the digests of every tied head the verifier retained, the shared prefix's head digest, the fork position (R7), and the count of rank-1 candidates the retention bound left unretained (R9; zero in every case but a rank-1 flood). `Inconclusive{cause: MissingEvents{range, failed_sources} | CopyDefect{at_event} | SingleSource{reached_sources}}` — the resolution delivered no key state the verifier may act on, for the named cause: the events linking a candidate to the accepted chain were not served, a served copy failed verification as a copy-level defect (R3), or a first contact reached fewer than the two relays R11 requires (R11). **Holding no cosigned head for an identity is not a cause here**, because no rule of this section reads one; a party that resolves an identity with no witness evidence returns `Confirmed` or `Adopted` and §9.11 records the absence of a fresh cosignature as that identity's standing (definitions). `Invalid{at_event}` — the chain fails R1–R4 at a named event by an author-attributable defect (R3); **this is the only cause of a resolution verdict named `Invalid`**, and a copy-level defect never produces this verdict. **A second result type carries the two other `Invalid` payloads this corpus writes, and this sentence names it so an SDK author has somewhere to put them:** `ContentVerdict` is what §9.7.1's content verification returns, and its `Invalid` variant carries `no_prefork_basis` (a member holding a `Contested` identity that observed no pre-fork Commit for it in this context, R7) and `no_boundary` (`03-identity.md` §3.2.1 step 5, `23-sync-and-offline-strategy.md`). **Those two are verdicts about one artifact inside one context and never about a chain**, so a resolution never returns them and this taxonomy never returns theirs; every SDK binding carries the two payload names unchanged, as it carries the six names above. `Discarded{accepted_head}` — the candidate is a head of the accepted chain at a sequence strictly lower than the accepted head, which the variant names; the verifier changed no stored state (R12). A discard is a verdict so that a consumer routes it, and §9.7.1 routes it as no refresh of the key state: it is not the success branch, because the resolution delivered nothing newer than the baseline the verifier already held. The contested verdict, the retained suffixes it rests on, and the accepted-head baseline MUST survive the verifier's restart and MUST be persisted before any verdict is acted on; a persist failure returns an error and not the verdict.

**Worked examples.** Notation: e0 is the inception event, which installs root R0 and fixes a commitment C over next set P (1-of-1 unless stated); a number is a sequence; the owner is the legitimate controller.

1. **A root thief forks early; the owner's device holding R0 is gone.** The owner's chain is e0 through e9 (R0-signed `KeyState` events). The thief, holding R0, forks at e5 and appends a5 through a50, all R0-signed. The owner no longer holds R0, so it signs a `RootRecovery{Lost}` at e10 that reveals C, installs a fresh root K′, carries a complete key state, and fixes C′. The shared prefix is e0 through e4 and its standing commitment is C. The owner's suffix reveals C but carries no R0 signature, so its rank is 2; the thief's suffix reveals nothing, so its rank is 3. The owner's chain wins under R6 wherever e10 sits, and the thief's head sequence 50 decides nothing. Under R8 the derived key state after e10 is e10's snapshot, so nothing the thief installed survives. **Had the owner still held a threshold of R0, it would have signed `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` and stood at rank 1 instead — a stronger position against any second claimant, and no different against this one.** The thief cannot evict the recovery by appending, because R9 never evicts a rank-1 suffix and evicts a rank-2 suffix only behind rank-3 candidates; nor can it make the recovery unpublishable by inflating its own chain, because the owner's segments attach by predecessor digest to e4, which every relay holding either chain already has. **The scope of that guarantee is a validating relay**: a non-validating relay and a foreign transport deliver availability and no eviction resistance, so a verifier querying only those may receive one chain.
2. **A copied pre-rotation key, the owner's root intact and trusted.** The owner's chain is e0 through e60 with R0 intact. The thief holds a copy of P and forks at e10 with a `RootRecovery{Lost}` a11, then appends to a50. The owner, suspecting the copy, signs a `CommitmentRollover` e61, which R3 makes R0 co-sign, revealing C and fixing C′. The shared prefix is e0 through e10. The owner's revealing event carries the signature of the root standing at its predecessor, so the owner's suffix is rank 1; the thief holds no root, so a11 carries none and the thief's suffix is rank 2. **The owner wins outright and the identity is not contested.** The rollover also fixes C′, which the thief cannot reveal, so the attempt cannot be repeated on the owner's suffix. **This is the case the root rule decides**, and it is why a controller that still holds its root signs a rollover rather than a `RootRecovery{Lost}`.
3. **Two root-only suffixes.** The owner's two devices both hold R0. Device A appends a `KeyState` e10a and device B a different `KeyState` e10b. Neither reveals C, so both suffixes are rank 3 and R7 makes the tie pending. R10's obligation is what avoids the tie: a device about to publish at a sequence the chain already carries an event at discards its own event, fetches the chain, and re-signs its intended change on the head it finds, at a cost of one event and no commitment. Where both devices published before either fetched, the owner ends the tie by signing a `CommitmentRollover` on the suffix it keeps, which puts that suffix at rank 1 — an exit that spends C and mints a spent key, which is why R10 states the avoidance first.
4. **A backup of a spent key later reaches the root thief.** The owner's chain carries a `RootRecovery{Lost}` r20 that revealed C_prev over P_prev and installed K′; the chain continues to e40. The thief, who holds R0 by r20's own premise, later obtains a backup of P_prev and forks at e19 with a `CommitmentRollover` a20 that reveals P_prev and carries R0's signature. The shared prefix is e0 through e19 and its standing root is R0. The thief's revealing event carries R0's signature, so the thief is rank 1; the owner's r20 carries no root signature, because the owner had already lost R0, so the owner is rank 2 and **the thief wins**. **This is the residual §9.7.4.1 item 4 discloses, and its precondition is the two losses together: the root gone, and a copyable copy of a spent key leaked.** On the consumer profile the controller holds no copy of a spent key to lose, because item 4's three methods are non-copyable. **Had the owner's r20 been a `RootRecovery{CoSigns}` — the row R10's table gives a controller that still holds a threshold of its root — the owner would stand at rank 1 and so would the thief**, because R6 reads the root standing at each revealing event's predecessor and the root standing at e19 is R0, which this row's own premise puts in the thief's hands. R7 would then return `Contested{tie: TerminalByKeyMaterial}`. **`CoSigns` upgrades this row's outcome from an outright loss to a terminal contest and never to a win**, and the owner listing R0 `Compromised{from: N}` in its own snapshot changes no rank, because R6 reads no key condition. A `CoSigns` recovery wins outright against a leaker that holds no root, which is item 4's table's right-hand column.
5. **An identity's witnesses go silent.** The owner's designated witnesses stop cosigning. **The chain keeps working and so does the identity's standing**: the owner publishes key events, every relying party verifies and adopts them under R1 through R4 and R12, the key state derives exactly as before because no rule of this section reads a cosignature, and each party corroborates the adopted head from the second source instead — its own read of that head from two community-relay-list entries under distinct declared operators (definitions). §9.11 therefore sets nothing, and the identity is admitted to contexts and granted UCANs as before. **What the owner loses is the portable half of its corroboration**, because a two-relay read is evidence to the party that performed it and to nobody else. A party that can reach only one such relay holds no corroboration at all, records that it holds none, and admits the identity and grants to it as before. The owner's remedy is a `KeyState` naming a fresh witness set (R10's table), which spends no commitment and takes effect the moment relying parties adopt it; each fresh witness seeds its baseline from the very event that designated it, verified from the inception event, and cosigns the chain that carries it (§9.7.4.3).
6. **A witness cosigns two divergent heads.** A party offers one witness the owner's chain and offers it, later, a fork. The witness's one check refuses the fork and it emits a conflict statement. A witness that instead cosigned both heads has departed from that check, and the two cosignatures prove it by themselves, because each names its predecessor (§9.7.4.3). **What that proof decides is the operator's conduct and never the fork**: a relying party holding both chains ranks them under R6 exactly as it would with no cosignature in hand, and it reads the pair as evidence that a party attempted a fork it was not online to observe.

### 9.7.4.3 Witness and Watcher Layer (Layer B)

**A witness watches and reports. It decides nothing.** Alec settled this on 2026-09-10 — "watch and report as well" — superseding the required-witnessing model of 2026-09-07, under which a threshold of witness cosignatures was a condition of an event's acceptability. **The record of that earlier model names its author, because the corpus once called it Alec's ruling and the decision log's own later entry corrects that:** he asked "So root, witness required, witness decides?", the orchestrator answered "root decides, witnesses required, witnesses never decide", and the log records that he did not confirm the "required" half in his own words. A witness runs one check, signs or refuses, and never adjudicates between two chains. **No rule of §9.7.4.2 reads a cosignature**: an event's validity, a suffix's rank, and a relying party's verdict are functions of the chain's own bytes. This section states the two objects, the one check, what a relying party may read a cosignature for, and where the recognized-operator list comes from. ADR-063, the inception-derived key-event-log identity substrate, settles this model and its amendment record carries Alec's reason.

**The cosigned head.** A cosigned head is one witness's signed statement that, at one moment, it held one identity's key-event chain at one head. Its fields, in preimage order:

| Field | Width | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `witness` | 32 bytes | The witness operator's inception-derived identifier (§9.7.4.2 R13) |
| `witness_key_state_head` | 32 bytes | The §9.5.1 preimage digest of the latest state-carrying event of the **witness's own** chain at or before the moment it signed, under §9.7.1's log-anchored evidence class |
| `subject` | 32 bytes | The cosigned identity's identifier |
| `sequence` | 8 bytes, big-endian u64 | The sequence of the head event |
| `event_digest` | 32 bytes | The §9.5.1 preimage digest of the head event |
| `previous_cosigned_digest` | 32 bytes | The preimage digest of this witness's immediately preceding cosigned head for this subject, or the all-zero placeholder for its first |
| `observed_at` | 8 bytes, big-endian u64 | Seconds since the Unix epoch, read from the witness's own clock |

The signature preimage is `SHA-256("SCP-COSIGNED-HEAD-V1:" || witness || witness_key_state_head || subject || sequence || event_digest || previous_cosigned_digest || observed_at)` under the §9.5.1 construction. Every field is fixed-width, so none carries a length prefix. The signature is one P-256 signature (§9.5) by the key the witness operator's key state lists `current` in the `#active` role at the position `witness_key_state_head` names. The object is 176 preimage bytes and one 64-byte signature, 240 bytes in all. **It covers the key-event log alone and says nothing about the identity's service record**, which `03-identity.md` §3.10.13 settles under its own sequence rules: covering that record would give a witness a second thing to check, which the watch-and-report ruling forbids.

**One cosignature covers every event at or below the head it names.** Each key event's preimage binds its predecessor's digest (§9.7.4.2 definitions), so the head's digest determines the whole chain back to the inception event and no second chain reaching that head exists without a SHA-256 collision. That is why a witness cosigns a head rather than receipting each event.

**Where the two objects live.** Cosigned heads are addressed at `wit_routing_id = SHA-256("scp:wit:" || subject_identifier_bytes)` and conflict statements at `SHA-256("scp:wcf:" || subject_identifier_bytes)`, beside `scp:did:` for the key-event record (§9.7.4.2 R13) and `scp:svc:` for the service record (`03-identity.md` §3.10.13). The address is the type discriminant (§9.10.12), so no decoder reads a cosigned head as a key-event frame.

**The one check.** Before it cosigns, a witness looks up the head it last cosigned for this subject, and the chain it is now asked to cosign MUST carry that exact event digest at that exact sequence. Where the chain carries it, the witness cosigns. Where the chain does not, the witness refuses and emits a conflict statement. **The check is unconditional and it has no carve-out**, including for a chain that supersedes the witness's last-cosigned chain through a reveal-authorized event: at a witness a genuine recovery and a theft of a spent pre-rotation key are the same object, and a witness holds no evidence that separates them. SCP's log is a hash chain rather than a Merkle tree, so the segment from the previously cosigned sequence to the new head is itself the consistency proof, which the witness verifies by walking predecessor digests. Two preconditions sit under the check and neither is a second judgement: the witness verifies the chain exactly as any relying party does, under §9.7.4.2 R2 and R1 through R4, and it refuses a subject whose key state at the head does not name it, because signing for undesignated subjects is an unbounded workload. Its `observed_at` MUST exceed that of its last cosigned head for the subject and MUST sit no further ahead of its own clock than §9.14's skew tolerance. **A witness whose stored `observed_at` exceeds its own clock by more than that tolerance keeps the record, refuses to cosign that subject, and reports the condition with its cause.** It does not re-seed: the trigger is a comparison against a clock no signature covers, so a party that moved that host's clock forward once and back could otherwise make a chosen witness discard the one value its check reads. Refusing is the direction every other clock reader in this design takes, and a present record is never discarded on a clock comparison.

**The witness's durable per-subject state.** A witness retains, per subject it has ever cosigned, the subject identifier, the sequence and event digest of the head it last cosigned, the preimage digest of that cosigned head, and its `observed_at`. Each record is about 112 bytes and a witness holds at most `MAX_SUBJECTS_PER_WITNESS` of them (§9.18.17), which bounds the store by construction. **The witness persists the record durably before it releases the cosignature that record accounts for.** A witness whose record for a subject is absent, or whose stored cosigned-head digest does not chain to the head it is about to sign, MUST NOT cosign that subject until it has re-seeded the baseline by the two-case rule below, because a witness cosigning on a cold baseline could sign both sides of a fork with no malice. `17-persistence-and-storage.md` §17.17.4 classifies an in-memory witness store as a security nullifier for that reason.

**Seeding a baseline reads the subject's own chain, and a re-seed reads the witness's own signatures.** Two cases, and no third.

- **A newly designated witness seeds from the event that designated it.** It verifies the offered chain from the inception event under R2 and R1 through R4, with R2's identifier recomputation; it locates the **first event on that chain whose key state names this witness in its witness set**; and it seeds its baseline at that event's sequence, that event's §9.5.1 preimage digest, and its own clock. It reads no cosigned head, its own or anyone's, and it counts no head another witness published. **A recovery therefore reaches a fresh set**: the recovery event is the event that designates them, it sits on the recovered suffix, and the outgoing witnesses' heads — which sit on a suffix the recovery does not carry — enter no fresh witness's floor.
- **A witness that lost its store re-seeds from its own previously published cosigned heads.** It fetches the cosigned heads at the subject's `scp:wit:` address, keeps only those whose `witness` field is itself and whose signature verifies under its own `#active` key at the position each object names, and seeds from the highest-sequence one of those. **It counts no other witness's head, at any sequence and at any age.** Finding none of its own, it holds no baseline for that subject and takes the first case above: it re-verifies the offered chain from the inception event and seeds from that chain's current event designating it.

**What the second case gives up, stated rather than implied.** A witness re-seeding from the designating event will cosign whichever chain carrying that event reaches it first, which is the same race item 2 of the closing list already states for a witness holding a baseline. That is the containment this layer accepts: no verdict, no rank, and no key-state derivation reads a cosignature, so a witness that cosigns the wrong side of a race hands no party the identity. The rule this replaces gave a fresh witness the highest-sequence head any witness of any set had ever published, which let a party that won one cosigning race set the floor for every witness the subject designated afterwards — and that floor is exactly what a recovery names a fresh set to escape.

**Which re-seed triggers a witness can detect, and which it cannot.** It detects two: a record absent for a subject it is asked to cosign, and a record whose stored cosigned-head digest does not match the `previous_cosigned_digest` of the head it is about to sign, which is a store that lost writes. **It detects neither a warm restart from an intact store nor a restore from a backup taken at an earlier sequence**, because both present a well-formed record with a past `observed_at` and a subject that has simply been quiet presents the same thing. This section states that rather than naming a trigger a witness cannot observe: `17-persistence-and-storage.md` §17.17.4 classifies an in-memory witness store as a security nullifier, and a durable store restored from an old backup is the residual that classification does not reach.


**A recovered identity reaches witnesses by naming a fresh set.** The outgoing witnesses hold a last-cosigned head the recovered suffix does not carry, so the one check makes them refuse it and go on refusing it. The recovery event names a fresh set; each of those witnesses holds no baseline for the subject, seeds at the recovery event itself under the first case above, and cosigns the chain that carries it. **A party holding a spent pre-rotation key takes the same path**, so both suffixes end up carrying cosignatures from disjoint sets and **neither set's cosignatures decide anything**: the relying party ranks the two suffixes under §9.7.4.2 R6's root rule, exactly as it would holding none. A controller whose witnesses merely went silent replaces them with a `KeyState`, which spends no commitment (§9.7.4.2 R10).

**The conflict statement.** When a witness refuses the one check it emits a conflict statement, which is this layer's fault proof. Its fields, in preimage order:

| Field | Width | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `witness` | 32 bytes | The refusing witness's identifier |
| `witness_key_state_head` | 32 bytes | The position that witness's own key state is read at |
| `subject` | 32 bytes | The subject identifier |
| `held_sequence` | 8 bytes, big-endian u64 | The sequence of the head this witness last cosigned |
| `held_digest` | 32 bytes | That head's event digest |
| `offered_sequence` | 8 bytes, big-endian u64 | The sequence at which the offered chain first differs |
| `offered_digest` | 32 bytes | The digest of the offered chain's event at that sequence |
| `observed_at` | 8 bytes, big-endian u64 | The witness's clock at refusal |

Its signature preimage is `SHA-256("SCP-WITNESS-CONFLICT-V1:" || witness || witness_key_state_head || subject || held_sequence || held_digest || offered_sequence || offered_digest || observed_at)` under the §9.5.1 construction, signed by the same key a cosigned head is signed by. The object is 184 preimage bytes and one 64-byte signature, 248 bytes in all.

**A conflict statement is portable evidence that two chains for one subject were offered to one named witness, and it is not a verdict.** The witness makes no claim about which chain the controller authored, and a party holding the statement applies R6 and R7 to the chains themselves. It exists rather than a silent refusal because a refusal producing no object would leave the fork attempt as the witness's private knowledge.

**`previous_cosigned_digest` is read here, and this is its one reader.** A witness's cosigned heads for one subject chain through that field, so **two of them naming the same `previous_cosigned_digest` and different `event_digest`s prove that the witness released two successors of one baseline** — a witness signing both sides of a fork, which its durable store and the one check together forbid. **The proof is portable and it is not self-contained**, and this states which: the two objects travel to any party, and verifying either signature takes the witness's own key state resolved at the position its `witness_key_state_head` names, under the non-recursive floor of §9.7.4.2's definitions. **That proof is about the operator's conduct and never about the fork**: a party holding it ranks the two chains under R6 exactly as it would without it.

**One party acts on that proof, and this names it rather than leaving the proof with no reader.** `18-addressability-and-deployment.md` §18.5.1 states that a party's recognized set changes at a release boundary and at no other moment, so the party that can act is the curator of the community relay list: **the curator MUST remove an operator's entry at the next release where a valid fault proof against that operator exists**, and an SDK MUST surface the fault proofs it holds so a curator can read them. Without that clause the proof is an invariant no party executes.

**The relay proof of control.** A community-relay-list entry declares an operator identity (`18-addressability-and-deployment.md` §18.5.1), and a **relay proof of control** is that operator's signed statement that the relay serving a query is the one its entry names. It lives in this section because this section already states the field set, the preimage, and the resolution rules of every object signed by a relay operator rather than by an identity's own keys. Its fields, in preimage order:

| Field | Width | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `operator` | 32 bytes | The operator's inception-derived identifier, as its community-relay-list entry declares it (§9.7.4.2 R13) |
| `operator_key_state_head` | 32 bytes | The §9.5.1 preimage digest of the latest state-carrying event of the **operator's own** chain at or before the moment it signed, under §9.7.1's log-anchored evidence class |
| `nonce` | 32 bytes | The requester's nonce, echoed verbatim from the QUERY that asked for this value (§9.10.12) |
| `routing_id` | 32 bytes | The routing id this response served — a `scp:did:`, `scp:wit:`, `scp:wcf:` or `scp:svc:` derivation (§9.7.4.2 R13, `03-identity.md` §3.10.13) |
| `value_digest` | 32 bytes | `SHA-256` over the concatenated blob bytes this response returned at that routing id, in the order it returned them |
| `observed_at` | 8 bytes, big-endian u64 | Seconds since the Unix epoch, read from the relay's own clock |

The signature preimage is `SHA-256("SCP-RELAY-PROOF-V1:" || operator || operator_key_state_head || nonce || routing_id || value_digest || observed_at)` under the §9.5.1 construction (§9.18.2). Every field is fixed-width, so none carries a length prefix. The signature is one P-256 signature (§9.5) by the key the operator's key state lists `current` in the `#active` role at the position `operator_key_state_head` names. The object is 168 preimage bytes and one 64-byte signature, 232 bytes in all.

**What a resolver checks, and the freshness bound.** A resolver counts a relay as a proven source only where all five hold: the `nonce` equals the nonce that resolver sent on that QUERY; the `routing_id` equals the one it queried; the `value_digest` equals `SHA-256` over the bytes it actually received; `observed_at` sits no further from the resolver's own clock, in either direction, than §9.14's skew tolerance; and the signature verifies against the operator's `#active` key, resolved from that operator's own chain by the non-recursive floor of §9.7.4.2's definitions. **The nonce is what makes a proof unreplayable**, and it is load-bearing: a proof signed over a static response and nothing else would let one party that once observed genuine responses from two listed relays present both to a reader on its own network path and satisfy a floor whose whole purpose is to defeat a single party in front of that reader. **A relay that serves no proof, or one that fails any of the five checks, counts as one unattributed source and can never be the second of two** (§9.7.4.2 R11).

**What a relying party may read a cosignature for.** Three things, and no fourth.

1. **Freshness, as one source of two.** A cosigned head from a recognized operator bounds how stale the head that party holds may be. **It is never the only source of it**: §9.7.4.2's definitions give a party a second and independent one, its own two-relay read of the adopted head. A cosignature therefore spares a party that read. **Its absence withholds nothing, closes no gate, and sets no standing** — §9.11 states that rule and this item cites it.
2. **First contact.** A party resolving an identity for the first time holds no reject-lower baseline, so a cosigned head from a recognized operator is evidence that operator served this chain at a stated time. It supplements R11's two-relay floor, replaces no part of it, and satisfies no part of it: R11's floor counts chains served under relay proofs of control, and a cosigned head is neither.
3. **Equivocation detection.** Two cosigned heads for one subject that neither extends the other, or a conflict statement, tell a party a fork exists. What it does with that is R6 and R7 on the chains themselves.

**Where the recognized set comes from.** `18-addressability-and-deployment.md` §18.5.1 defines the community relay list — a per-release artifact, identical in every binding, each entry declaring the operator identity that runs the relay — and that list is the SDK's default recognized set (§9.7.4.2 definitions). A cosignature from an operator the running SDK does not list counts toward nothing and is not an error, so **a release boundary changes what evidence a party holds and changes no verdict it reaches**. **A cosignature proves control of its declared operator identity by construction**, because producing one requires that operator's own `#active` key.

**Independence of an operator from a subject's controller is not provable, and no sentence in this corpus implies a check covers it** (Alec, 2026-09-07).
